A positive regulon score was then computed for each cell using RegulonScores, and the scaled average regulon score per cell identity was plotted using pheatmap76.

Three published Arabidopsis root single-cell datasets were used to verify that the ERS and associated ERS-specific genes described in this study are also present in other datasets7,8,16. For ref. 8, we directly used the processed Seurat object provided by the authors. For ref. 7, we reprocessed the raw fastq files using the same pre-processing steps as described for our Col-0 atlas. For ref. 16, we used the count matrices provided by the authors and performed batch integration.

Once the final Seurat objects were generated, cells were annotated using our reference atlas following the same procedure described in the section ‘Cell annotation’ above. Cells were also projected onto the atlas UMAP embedding using the MapQuery function in Seurat (v.5.0.0). Co-expression gene modules were projected onto these datasets using the ProjectModules function in hdWGCNA (v.0.4.1), and module scores were calculated with ModuleExprScore using n_genes = “all” and method = “Seurat”.

To assess the robustness of ERS assignments in external datasets, label transfer was repeated across a range of Seurat parameter settings using the integrated Col-0 atlas as reference. Specifically, projections were performed using 30, 40 or 50 principal components and k.anchor values of 5, 10 or 20, which generated nine parameter combinations in total. For each parameter set, transfer anchors were identified using FindTransferAnchors with the RPCA integrated embedding, and cell identities were assigned using TransferData. To focus analyses on cells robustly assigned to the ERS bifurcation, only cells classified as ERS+ or ERS− in the baseline parameter set (50 principal components, k.anchor = 5) were retained for downstream comparisons. Predicted identities were separated into cell-type and ERS-state components, and stability was assessed independently for both. Cell-type stability was quantified as the fraction of parameter sets in which each cell retained its baseline cell-type assignment, whereas ERS stability was calculated as the consistency of ERS+ and ERS− assignment in the baseline cell type across parameter sets. Global agreement between parameter sets was further quantified using pairwise adjusted rand index (ARI) comparisons across all transferred labels. Furthermore, the proportion of ERS+ cells in each cell type, the fraction of cells retaining their baseline cell type assignment and the frequency of ERS state switching across parameter sets were calculated independently for each external dataset.

The fragment files generated with Cell Ranger ATAC (v.1.2) and mapped to TAIR10/Araport11 reference were obtained from a previous study16, imported to snapatac2 (v.2.6.4) and paired-insertions calculated77 in 500 bp bins78. The cells were initially defined as barcodes associated with more than 3,500 and fewer than 50,000 fragments per barcode and with a TSS enrichment score higher than 2.5. Low-quality cells and outliers were also identified per-sample basis using the 3× median absolute deviation threshold and further removed from analysis79. Following peak calling using macs3 (ref. 80), peak and gene activity matrices were calculated using the paired-insertion counting strategy. For ATAC–seq data integration into our scRNA-seq dataset, the peak matrices were imported to the Signac package81 and the dimensions of ATAC–seq were reduced using latent semantic indexing. ATAC–seq nuclei were assigned identities using our reference atlas and the FindTransferAnchors function with the parameters reduction = ‘cca’ and dims = 1:30, followed by TransferData with weight.reduction = obj[[“lsi”]] and dims = 2:30. The TransferData function and previously computed anchors were also used to generate an ‘imputed’ scRNA-seq matrix for the ATAC nuclei. Using this imputed matrix, the snATAC–seq and scRNA-seq reference atlases were merged into a single object, processed with ScaleData and RunPCA, and the first 30 principal components were used to produce a UMAP co-embedding of the two datasets.

To perform differential expression analysis of our co-expression modules, a Seurat object was created with all conditions to compare, and the data were scaled using ScaleData (Seurat v.5.0.0). The modules were projected into the new dataset using ProjectModules (hdWGCNA v.0.4.1). A module score was then calculated using ModuleExprScore. The differential module scores were determined using the FindDMEs function with features = “ModuleScores” and test.use = “wilcox”. The results were plotted using PlotDMEsLollipop.

All changes in cell composition were tested with sccomp (v.1.99.18) in R82. Specifically, when evaluating changes in ERS+ (and consequently ERS−) proportions, the Seurat object was subsetted to retain only cells that had reached the developmental stage at which they could be assigned to either state to avoid taking into account sampling effects that might affect the total number of mature cells captured. This process excluded the root cap, ‘early’ identities and the non-hair cells, for which the trajectories could determine the point of branching. The test was then run on the subsetted data using sccomp_estimate and sccomp_test, and results were plotted with sccomp_boxplot or with plot in the case of the water-deficit experiment for which the covariate is continuous.

Seedlings were grown vertically on full (1×) or half (0.5×) MS plates, as for scRNA-seq experiments. Pictures of the roots were taken at 6 d.a.g., using a stereomicroscope (n = 16 for full MS, n = 23 for half MS, from two different plates each). A portion corresponding to approximately 2.5 mm from the tip was selected for each image of the root, and root hairs were manually counted using the Cell Counter plugin in Fiji. A significant difference in root hair density between the two conditions was determined using a Wilcoxon rank-sum test (wilcox.test function) in R.

Note that the seedlings used for this quantification were in the top row of the plate (seeds were sown in two rows for scRNA-seq experiments). Roots of seedlings grown in the bottom row tended to have fewer root hairs (in both conditions).

A new Seurat object was created using the Col-0 cells that were grown on full MS (which is all replicates except the three half MS replicates), as well as the cells of the C24, Cvi-0, Ler and Ws-2 atlases. The expression data were then pseudobulked per genotype, cell identity and batch using the AggregateExpression in Seurat (v.5.0.0). For each possible inter-accession replicate pair, a differential expression test was performed on the pseudobulk data using FindMarkers with DESeq2. Genes were considered differentially expressed if the adjusted P < 0.05 and the absolute log2 fold-change was >0.5. For a gene to be called an accession-specific DEG, it had to be systematically differentially expressed in a given accession compared with the other four, but not be differentially expressed in any pairwise comparison between the remaining four accessions. The proportion of ecotype-specific DEGs per identity or per module were plotted using pheatmap.

Module scores for hdWGCNA modules 20 and 21 were calculated using AddModuleScore in Seurat (v.5.0.0). Analyses were restricted to cells classified as ERS+ or ERS− from the 100% and 20% water conditions. Total UMI counts were log10-transformed after addition of a pseudocount. Module scores were analysed using linear mixed-effects models implemented in the lme4 (v.1.1.36) and lmerTest (v.3.2.1) R packages, with water condition as a fixed effect, log10 UMI count modelled using a natural spline with four degrees of freedom (splines v.4.3.1 package) and biological replicate included as a random intercept. For visualization, cells were divided into ten equal-frequency bins according to log10 UMI count, and mean module scores ± s.e.m. were calculated in each bin. Binning was used for visualization only; all statistical inference was performed on the unbinned cell-level data. The association between water condition and ERS identity was assessed using binomial generalized linear mixed-effects models (glmer; lme4) with biological replicate included as a random intercept. An unadjusted model included water condition alone, whereas the adjusted model also included log10 UMI count as a fixed-effect covariate. Odds ratios and 95% confidence intervals for the effect of water condition were obtained using broom.mixed (v.0.2.9.6).

To compare roots grown under different conditions using whole-mount HCR, all roots were grown, processed and imaged simultaneously using identical confocal settings.

First, a mask defining the cytoplasmic area of the root was created in Fiji. A threshold-based mask of the whole root was generated from the calcofluor channel using Image > Adjust > Threshold, followed by Process > Binary > Close and Process > Binary > Fill Holes. A second threshold-based mask was created from the calcofluor channel to define cell walls. Subtracting the cell wall mask from the whole-root mask (Process > Math > Subtract) produced a mask containing only the cytoplasmic regions. This mask was then multiplied by a mask selecting the region from the elongation zone upwards, corresponding to the area where ERS expression begins in single-cell data.

The resulting cytoplasmic mask was multiplied by the channel containing the HCR signal to retain only signal in cells from the elongation zone upwards. A separate threshold-based mask was created from the HCR signal channel to identify areas above a defined signal threshold. Multiplying this mask by the cytoplasmic mask ensured that only signal inside cells from the elongation zone upwards was considered.

Custom Fiji macros were used to automate measurements across z-stacks. One macro measured the surface area covered by a mask in each slice, which enabled calculation of the total cytoplasmic area for normalization and the area of positive signal. Another macro measured the sum of all pixel intensities per slice, which was applied to the cytoplasmic signal image.

Differences in signal between conditions were assessed using the Wilcoxon rank-sum test.

Single-cell segmentation was performed in 3D using the cell-wall staining channel. Individual cells were first manually seeded by placing markers in each cell lumen and storing these positions as regions of interest. A binary marker image was then generated from the seed positions. The cell wall channel was processed using the MorphoLibJ83 Morphological Filters (3D) function with the Gradient filter to enhance cell boundaries. The processed cell-wall image and binary marker image were subsequently used for marker-controlled watershed segmentation using MorphoLibJ. Segmentation outputs were manually curated to correct improper segmentations caused by discontinuities or holes in cell-wall staining, either by removing incorrectly segmented cells or merging oversegmented regions. Before fluorescence quantification, signal channels were background-corrected to minimize the contribution of imaging noise and diffuse background fluorescence. In brief, pixel intensities were measured in a region outside the root tissue, and the 99th percentile background intensity value was subtracted from all pixels in the image using the Subtract function in Fiji. The resulting filtered fluorescence images were combined with the curated segmentation masks to quantify volumetric single-cell mean fluorescence intensity using the Intensity Measurements 2D/3D function in MorphoLibJ. Measured fluorescence values were then mapped back onto segmented cells using the Assign Measure to Labels function in MorphoLibJ to generate colour-coded single-cell fluorescence representations.

For the water-deficit HCR experiments, segmentation was restricted to epidermal and cortical cells. Fluorescence quantification was performed on the original, non-background-subtracted images so that identical image processing was applied across control and water-deficit samples, which enabled direct comparison of fluorescence intensities between conditions. Mean fluorescence intensity was measured for each segmented cell. To account for batch-to-batch differences in HCR signal intensity, the mean fluorescence intensity for each segmented cell was normalized to the mean per-cell fluorescence of the corresponding 100% water control sample processed in parallel. Empirical cumulative distribution functions were then generated to compare the distributions of normalized single-cell fluorescence intensities between conditions.

To test whether mutually exclusive marker expression defined two separable cell populations, Gaussian mixture modelling was applied independently to fluorescence quantification data and scRNA-seq expression data using the R package mclust84 (v.6.0.0). For fluorescence imaging data, mean single-cell fluorescence intensities for AT3G01720 and AT5G56550 were extracted from segmented cells following background correction and volumetric quantification. A marker ratio was calculated for each cell as AT3G01720/(AT3G01720 + AT5G56550), and Gaussian mixture modelling was performed on the resulting distribution using Mclust. Cells were assigned to the inferred populations based on maximum posterior probability. The fitted Gaussian components and their relative proportions were visualized over the observed ratio distribution. For scRNA-seq data, normalized expression values for AT3G01720 and AT5G56550 were extracted from hair cells in the reference atlas. Cells with zero combined expression for both markers were excluded from the analysis. A marker score was then calculated as the difference between AT3G01720 and AT5G56550 expression values, and Gaussian mixture modelling was applied to the score distribution using Mclust. Fitted Gaussian distributions were visualized together with the observed score distribution to assess the presence of two transcriptionally distinct populations.

a, UMAP plots showing the expression of previously characterised marker genes associated with distinct stages and subdomains of root cap development, including CDF4 (differentiating columella), FEZ (early root cap), RCPG (root cap tip), and BFN1 (lateral programmed cell death zone), validating the biological interpretation of the inferred root cap trajectories based on their known in vivo expression patterns during root cap development13,14,15. b–d, Validation of pseudotime assignments by comparison of in silico expression patterns and whole-mount HCR in intact roots. In the endodermis (b), AT2G14900, predicted to be expressed early according to the trajectory analysis, is detected closer to the meristem, whereas AT2G36100, a later marker, is detected further away, consistent with the inferred developmental progression. Similarly, in the xylem pole pericycle (c), AT2G18800 (early marker) is detected nearer to the meristem and AT3G47510 (late marker) further away. In the epidermis (d), AT1G66470 (early marker) and AT4G40090 (late marker) also show concordant expression patterns in silico and in vivo. Dotted lines indicate the earliest developmental stage at which expression of each marker was detected. Multiple optical sections are shown when a complete longitudinal section of the root could not be obtained at once due to minor root curvature during mounting. Representative images are shown; results were reproduced in at least two independent experiments with similar results. Scale bar = 50 μm. e, UMAP plot summarising the diffusion pseudotime assignment for each lineage in the reference atlas.

a, Scatter plots and regression lines showing the distribution of expression scores from our Col-0 scRNA-seq atlas for the top 50 genes associated with the meristematic, elongation, and maturation zones defined by Shahan et al.8, plotted as a function of diffusion pseudotime for each cell type. Successive peaks of meristematic, elongation, and maturation scores are observed along the pseudotime axis, consistent with the expected developmental progression and validating the inferred trajectories. b–e, Monocle trajectory analysis. b, UMAP plot showing the partitions defined by Monocle, for which independent trajectory analyses were computed. Endodermis and cortex, xylem and phloem pole pericycle, and procambium and phloem are grouped within the same partition. c, UMAP plot showing the pseudotime ordering obtained with Monocle. Arrows indicate shifts in inferred t = 0 placement relative to the reference atlas. d, UMAP plot showing the resulting Monocle trajectories, recovering the same two branches identified across cell types by the PAGA trajectory analysis. e, Scatter plots showing Monocle pseudotime (scaled from 0 to 1) and diffusion pseudotime assignment per cell, by cell type, demonstrating strong concordance between the two methods. Spearman correlation coefficients and cell numbers are indicated in the upper left of each panel. f–g, RNA velocity analysis using scVelo. f, UMAP plot showing the latent time assignment obtained with scVelo. Arrows indicate shifts in inferred t = 0 placement relative to the reference atlas. g, Scatter plots showing scVelo latent time and diffusion pseudotime assignment per cell, by cell type. Spearman correlation coefficients and cell numbers are indicated in the upper left of each panel. Lower correlations in some lineages are primarily explained by slight differences in inferred t = 0 placement between the velocity analysis and the other trajectory inference methods.

a–c, Cell cycle analysis. a, UMAP plot showing the predicted cell cycle phase of cells in the reference atlas. b, Bar plot showing the proportion of cells in each predicted cell cycle phase per cluster. c, UMAP plot of the reference atlas after regression of cell cycle effects, showing that the bifurcating trajectories are maintained. d, UMAP plot showing cells in the reference atlas coloured by experimental batch. e, Box plots showing the distribution of the log2 fold-change in expression following protoplast isolation for genes belonging to the co-expression modules identified in Fig. 2b,c (n = 8 biologically independent samples per condition over 2 independent experiments). Branch 1-specific modules 20 and 21 are not preferentially up- or down-regulated during protoplast isolation. Positive fold-change values indicate higher transcript abundance in protoplasts. f–h, Analysis of roots grown penetrating vertically through the agar rather than along the agar surface. f, Diagram illustrating the two experimental growth conditions. g, UMAP plot showing cell identity assignments for roots grown penetrating through agar using the reference atlas. The bifurcating trajectories are also detected in this dataset. h, Projection of cells from roots penetrating through agar onto the reference atlas UMAP embedding, showing that cells distribute across all branches similarly to the reference atlas. i,j, Distribution of the percentage of mitochondrial (i) and chloroplast (j) transcripts in cells assigned to ‘branch 1’ (ERS+), ‘branch 2’ (ERS−), or without branch assignment (root cap and early pre-branching cells) (n = 87,056 cells from the reference atlas across 9 independent experiments). Only a few dozen cells in the >87,000-cell reference atlas exceed 5%, a commonly used threshold for low-quality cells. ‘Branch 2’ cells, despite their lower UMI content, show mitochondrial and chloroplast transcript proportions comparable to root cap and early cells, suggesting they are unlikely to represent damaged or low-quality cells. For e, i and j, box plots show the median (centre), 25th–75th percentiles (box), and smallest and largest values within 1.5× the interquartile range (whiskers).

a–c, Analysis of gene expression differences between ‘branch 1’ (ERS+) and ‘branch 2’ (ERS−) in the xylem pole pericycle. a, Scatter plot showing the average expression in ‘branch 1’ versus ‘branch 2’ cells for genes from two categories of co-expression modules: ‘branch 1’-specific modules (Modules 20 and 21; n = 935 genes) and modules expressed in both branches (Modules 13 and 14; n = 329 genes). The dotted line indicates the identity line (x = y), and the solid black line indicates the linear regression fitted using genes expressed in both branches. Dashed vertical lines indicate the overlapping range of ‘branch 1’ expression between the two gene categories, defined from the common 10th to 90th percentile range and used for the controlled analysis in panel c. b, Distribution of average expression in ‘branch 1’ cells for the two gene categories (n = 935 genes for Modules 20 and 21; n = 329 genes for Modules 13 and 14). Dashed horizontal lines indicate the same overlapping expression range shown in panel a. Although genes expressed in both branches extend further into the upper tail of the distribution, both gene categories include genes within the same lower expression range. c, Residual ‘branch 2’ expression, calculated as the observed ‘branch 2’ expression minus the expression expected from the regression fitted in panel a. Residuals are shown for genes within the overlapping ‘branch 1’ expression range indicated in panels a and b (n = 747 genes for Modules 20 and 21; n = 209 genes for Modules 13 and 14). ‘Branch 1’-specific genes show significantly lower residual ‘branch 2’ expression than genes expressed in both branches (two-sided Wilcoxon rank-sum test, p-value < 0.0001), indicating that their absence from ‘branch 2’ cannot be explained solely by lower expression or reduced transcript detection in cells with lower UMI content. d–g, UMI downsampling analysis. d,e, Distribution of UMI content per cell identity in the reference atlas before (d) and after (e) UMI downsampling (n = 87,056 cells from the reference atlas across 9 independent experiments). UMI content was downsampled in ‘branch 1’ (ERS+) cells to span the same range as their ‘branch 2’ (ERS−) counterparts. f,g, UMAP plots showing cell identities (f) and meristematic score used for pseudotime assignment (g) after three independent UMI downsampling computations. Bifurcating trajectories are detected in all three downsampling replicates, with cells showing the highest meristematic score per lineage consistently positioned between branches, as in the reference atlas. For b–e, box plots show the median (centre), 25th–75th percentiles (box), and smallest and largest values within 1.5× the interquartile range (whiskers).

a, UMAPs showing our reference atlas and the projection of nuclei from Farmer et al.16 into the atlas UMAP embedding, with cells coloured by their assigned identity based on our atlas. b, UMAP showing the distribution of UMI content per nucleus in Farmer et al. c–f, UMAPs (c,d) and violin plots (e,f) showing the distribution of expression scores for our ‘branch 1’-specific modules 20 and 21 in the snRNA-seq data from Farmer et al. g–k, Label transfer stability analysis in the Farmer et al. dataset. g, Parameter combinations used for label transfer stability analysis, showing the nine combinations of dimensionality and k.anchor values tested. h, Proportion of ‘branch 1’ (ERS+) cells within each cell type across all parameter combinations (n = 9 combinations). Box plots show the median (centre), 25th–75th percentiles (box), and smallest and largest values within 1.5× the interquartile range (whiskers). i, Pairwise Adjusted Rand Index (ARI) comparisons between all parameter combinations for transferred labels, showing strong global agreement across projections. j, Fraction of cells retaining their baseline cell type assignment across parameter combinations. Dashed line indicates the baseline parameter set used throughout the study (50 principal components, k.anchor = 5; condition 3). k, Proportion of cells retaining the same branch (ERS) state, switching branch (ERS) state, or losing branch (ERS) assignment relative to the baseline parameter set. The vast majority of cells retained both their cell type identity and branch (ERS) state assignment across parameter combinations.

a, UMAPs showing our reference atlas and the projection of cells from Wendrich et al.7 into the atlas UMAP embedding, with cells coloured by their assigned identity based on our atlas. b, UMAP showing whether cells were originally discarded (‘filtered’) in their study based on UMI content. c, UMAP showing the distribution of UMI content per cell. d–g, UMAPs (d,e) and violin plots (f,g) showing the distribution of expression scores for our ‘branch 1’-specific modules 20 and 21 in the scRNA-seq data from Wendrich et al. h–l, Label transfer stability analysis in the Wendrich et al. dataset. h, Parameter combinations used for label transfer stability analysis, showing the nine combinations of dimensionality and k.anchor values tested. i, Proportion of ‘branch 1’ (ERS+) cells within each cell type across all parameter combinations. (n = 9 combinations). Box plots show the median (centre), 25th–75th percentiles (box), and smallest and largest values within 1.5× the interquartile range (whiskers). j, Pairwise Adjusted Rand Index (ARI) comparisons between all parameter combinations for transferred labels, showing strong global agreement across projections. k, Fraction of cells retaining their baseline cell type assignment across parameter combinations. Dashed line indicates the baseline parameter set used throughout the study (50 principal components, k.anchor = 5; condition 3). l, Proportion of cells retaining the same branch (ERS) state, switching branch (ERS) state, or losing branch (ERS) assignment relative to the baseline parameter set. The vast majority of cells retained both their cell type identity and branch (ERS) state assignment across parameter combinations.

a, UMAPs showing the reference atlas embedding from Shahan et al.8, with cells coloured by their assigned identity based on our atlas. b, UMAP showing the distribution of UMI content per cell in their atlas. c–f, UMAPs (c,d) and violin plots (e,f) showing the distribution of expression scores for our ‘branch 1’-specific modules 20 and 21 in the scRNA-seq data from Shahan et al. g–k, Label transfer stability analysis in the Shahan et al. dataset. g, Parameter combinations used for label transfer stability analysis, showing the nine combinations of dimensionality and k.anchor values tested. h, Proportion of ‘branch 1’ (ERS+) cells within each cell type across all parameter combinations (n = 9 combinations). Box plots show the median (centre), 25th–75th percentiles (box), and smallest and largest values within 1.5× the interquartile range (whiskers). i, Pairwise Adjusted Rand Index (ARI) comparisons between all parameter combinations for transferred labels, showing strong global agreement across projections. j, Fraction of cells retaining their baseline cell type assignment across parameter combinations. Dashed line indicates the baseline parameter set used throughout the study (50 principal components, k.anchor = 5; condition 3). k, Proportion of cells retaining the same branch (ERS) state, switching branch (ERS) state, or losing branch (ERS) assignment relative to the baseline parameter set. The vast majority of cells retained both their cell type identity and branch (ERS) state assignment across parameter combinations.

a, UMAP plots showing the atlases from C24, Cvi-0, Ler, and Ws-2 cells, coloured by identity assigned using our reference atlas. b,c, UMAP plots showing the expression patterns of the ‘branch 1’-specific gene modules (Modules 20 and 21) identified in our Col-0 reference atlas in the other four ecotypes, demonstrating the same enrichment in ‘branch 1’ cells. d, Box plots showing the proportion of ‘branch 1’ (ERS+) and consequently ‘branch 2’ (ERS−) cells in atlases from all five tested ecotypes (Col-0, n = 6 biologically independent experiments; C24, Cvi-0, Ler and Ws-2, n = 3 biologically independent experiments each). Colours indicate branch proportions significantly different from Col-0. e, Heatmap showing the number of ecotype-specific up- or down-regulated genes. f, Heatmap showing the percentage of ecotype-specific up- or down-regulated genes per gene co-expression module.

a, UMAP plot showing the co-embedding of our scRNA-seq reference atlas and the snATAC-seq data from Farmer et al.16, obtained by imputing RNA expression for each nucleus. Cells/nuclei are coloured by assay type or imputed cell identity. b, Box plots showing the distribution of the fraction of reads in peaks (FRIP) per assigned identity for snATAC-seq nuclei (n = 7,207 nuclei from 2 independent experiments). c, Box plots showing the distribution of transcription start site enrichment (TSSE) per assigned identity for snATAC-seq nuclei (n = 7,207 nuclei from 2 independent experiments). d,e, Box plots showing the expression score for the two ‘branch 1’-specific modules 20 (d) and 21 (e) in snATAC-seq nuclei, per assigned identity (n = 7,207 nuclei from 2 independent experiments). For b–e, box plots show the median (centre), 25th–75th percentiles (box), and smallest and largest values within 1.5× the interquartile range (whiskers).

a, Longitudinal optical sections of roots used to visualise the expression of the four branch 1-specific genes shown in Fig. 3a. Yellow rectangles mark the portions depicted in Fig. 3a. Scale bar = 50 μm. b, Expression of the branch 1-specific gene AT4G24060 (VDOF1). Left, UMAP plot showing the branch 1-specific expression of VDOF1 in silico. Right, longitudinal optical section showing VDOF1 expression by whole-mount HCR. Cells enriched (+) or not (−) for VDOF1 transcripts in the endodermis (en.) or pericycle (pe.) are indicated by arrows. Scale bar = 50 μm. c, Validation that absence of branch 1 marker signal in negative cells is not due to failed HCR detection. Top, UMAP plots showing the expression patterns of the ‘branch 1’-associated marker AT2G41660 (MIZ1) and endodermal marker AT2G14900 in the reference atlas. AT2G41660 is enriched specifically in branch 1 endodermal cells, whereas AT2G14900 is expressed throughout the endodermis independently of branch state. Bottom, representative whole-mount HCR images showing simultaneous detection of AT2G41660 (magenta) and AT2G14900 (cyan) in Arabidopsis root endodermal cells. While AT2G41660 signal is restricted to a subset of endodermal cells, consistent with branch 1-associated expression, AT2G14900 is robustly detected in both AT2G41660-positive and AT2G41660-negative cells. Arrows indicate examples of endodermal cells lacking detectable AT2G41660 signal but retaining clear AT2G14900 expression. These results demonstrate that the absence of branch 1 marker signal reflects genuine biological variation rather than differences in probe performance or hybridisation efficiency. Scale bar = 50 μm. The HCR experiments shown in a–c were independently repeated at least twice with similar results.

a, Box plots showing the distribution of ERS+ and ERS− cell proportions per branching cell type in the reference Col-0 atlas and in the two ABA-related mutants, aba2-3 and pyl11458 (Col-0, n = 6 biologically independent experiments; aba2-3 and pyl11458, n = 3 biologically independent experiments each). Each dot represents an individual replicate. Tests showing a significant difference in cell state proportion compared to Col-0 are highlighted in pink (sccomp, FDR < 0.05). b–d, Analysis of the scr-3 mutant. b, UMAP plots showing our reference atlas and the projection of cells from scr-3 replicates into the atlas UMAP embedding, with cells coloured by their assigned identity based on our atlas. Consistent with previous reports8, the single ground tissue layer initially exhibits a cortex-like identity before progressively adopting endodermal identity. c, scr-3 cells shown in their atlas UMAP embedding. d, Box plots showing the distribution of cell types in the reference Col-0 atlas and in the scr-3 mutant (Col-0, n = 6 biologically independent experiments; scr-3, n = 3 biologically independent experiments). Each dot represents an individual replicate. Tests showing a significant difference in cell type proportion compared to Col-0 are highlighted in pink (sccomp, FDR < 0.05). Although columella and pericycle identities are strongly depleted, the corresponding cell layers remain physically present. Conversely, adjacent cell identities are enriched, raising the possibility that these cells have adopted neighbouring identities.

a,b, Root phenotypes and ERS proportions in full MS versus half MS conditions. a, Top, representative images of roots grown vertically on full MS or half MS plates, 6 days after germination. Scale bar = 1 mm. Bottom, box plot showing the distribution of the number of root hairs in the first 2.5 mm of roots grown on full MS or half MS (full MS, n = 16 roots; half MS, n = 23 roots; two plates per condition). Asterisks indicate p-value < 0.001 (two-sided Wilcoxon rank-sum test). b, Box plots showing the distribution of ERS+ and ERS− cell proportions per branching cell type in three replicates grown simultaneously on either full MS or half MS medium (n = 3 biologically independent experiments per condition). Tests showing a significant difference in cell state proportion between conditions are highlighted in pink (sccomp, FDR < 0.05). c,d, Cell type and ERS state distributions under the “hard agar” drought model; lines and bands indicate estimates and 95% credible intervals (sccomp). c, Scatter plots with regression lines showing the distribution of root cap cells, early developmental-stage cells prior to ERS+/− branching (‘epidermis early’, ‘hair cells early’, and ‘protoxylem early’), and cells that have reached the ERS+/− stage across 100%, 50% and 20% H2O. Significant changes are highlighted by rectangles (sccomp, FDR < 0.05). d, ERS+ and ERS− cell proportions per branching cell type across the same conditions. Significant changes are highlighted by rectangles (sccomp, FDR < 0.05). e, UMI counts in ERS−, ERS+ and non-ERS cells under 100% H2O (n = 25,964 cells, 2 independent experiments) and 20% H2O (n = 12,882 cells, 2 independent experiments). Violin plots show cell-level distributions. FC, fold change. f, Mean ERS module 20/21 scores across log10 UMI bins; points show means and error bars indicate s.e.m. Linear mixed-effects models tested differences between water conditions while adjusting for log10 UMI counts (p-value < 1 × 10−4, module 20; p-value = 0.016, module 21; 100% H2O, n = 14,771 cells; 20% H2O, n = 4,962 cells; 2 independent experiments per condition; two-sided tests). g, Odds ratios (OR) for ERS+ classification from binomial generalized linear mixed-effects models before and after log10 UMI adjustment. Points indicate OR estimates and horizontal lines indicate 95% confidence intervals (CI): OR = 3.2 (95% CI, 2.2–4.8; p-value < 1 × 10−4) unadjusted and OR = 2.0 (95% CI, 1.4–3.1; p-value = 0.00061) after adjustment (same n as f; two-sided tests). h, Proportion of cells assigned as ERS+ plotted against the proportion of cells filtered during preprocessing for each library. No correlation is observed between these two metrics, indicating that measured changes in ERS+ proportions are not driven by differential filtering of low-UMI ERS− cells. i, Increased ERS marker expression under drought conditions is not explained by reduced root size. Left, representative whole-mount HCR images showing expression of the ERS marker AT3G04010 in control roots at 3 and 6 days after germination (dag). Despite the substantially smaller size of 3 dag roots, fluorescence intensity at the root tip remains comparable between stages. Right, quantification of AT3G04010 HCR signal intensity normalised by root area in 3 dag and 6 dag control roots (n = 2 independent biological samples per condition from one experiment). Similar fluorescence levels were observed at both developmental stages, indicating that reduced root size does not explain the increased ERS-associated expression observed under water-deficit conditions. Scale bar = 50 μm. For a, b and e, box plots show the median (centre), 25th–75th percentiles (box), and smallest and largest values within 1.5× the interquartile range (whiskers).

#1487. Official: Dybala left out of Roma squad for Cagliari clash

Paulo Dybala is still not ready to return from injury, so Roma coach Gian Piero Gasperini has kept him off the Giallorossi’s squad list for tonight’s match against Cagliari.

Roma star Dybala remains on the sidelines for tonight’s Serie A match against Cagliari.

The Giallorossi have just announced their squad list for the game, and Dybala isn’t in it.

The team trained at Trigoria this morning, hours before kick-off at 20:45 CET [19:45 GMT], and at the end of the session, Gasperini decided not to include the Argentine in the available players list.

According to Sky Sport Italia, Dybala still needs a few more training sessions before being eligible to feature in a competitive match.

Roma have a tight fixture list, as tonight’s game is their sixth since club football returned in early October after a three-week international break.

Dybala, 32, has provided four assists in six appearances across all competitions this season and missed the last three games with a calf injury.

After tonight’s home Serie A game with Cagliari, Roma will visit Udinese next Saturday and then meet Manchester United away on Tuesday, November 3. According to Sky, Roma hope Dybala will be available against Udinese and fit enough to start against the Red Devils at Old Trafford.

Defenders: Rensch, Koulierakis, Ndicka, Molina, Hermoso, Mancini, Balerdi, Wesley, Lulli, Ghilardi

Midfielders: Cristante, De Roon, Pisilli, Di Nunzio, Mora

Lorenzo Bettoni is the Editor of Football Italia.

#1486. Arsenal to re-issue iconic 90s kit as part of retro collection

Arsenal are set to bring back their classic 1992-94 home shirt as part of an upcoming retro collection from adidas, which also includes a special edition of the adidas Gazelle.

Footy Headlines state the drop is scheduled for October 2025.

The shirt itself is a faithful reproduction of the original design, featuring the signature red base with faint striping, white sleeves, the JVC sponsor and a centralised club crest. It was in this kit that the Gunners lifted both the FA Cup in 1993 and the Cup Winnersâ Cup in 1994, etching it into club folklore.

#1485. England captain Harry Kane's camp 'increasingly confident' he will be crowned world's best footballer TONIGHT, Spanish report claims

Published: 11:03 GMT, 26 October 2026 | Updated: 14:05 GMT, 26 October 2026

Harry Kane's camp are increasingly confident that he will lift the Ballon d'Or tonight, according to a report in Spain.

The Bayern Munich striker is among the favourites to lift the gong in London alongside Barcelona's Lamine Yamal and Real Madrid's Kylian Mbappe.

After scoring 73 goals for club and country in the 2025-26 season, he is gunning to become the first English winner since Michael Owen in 2001.

'The feeling surrounding Harry Kane is that, all things considered, his candidacy is the strongest,' write Spanish outlet Marca.

Kane helped fire England to the World Cup semi-finals and also won the Bundesliga, DFB-Pokal and German Supercup with Bayern.

The most prestigious individual honours in world football will be awarded tonight, with the likes of Lionel Messi, Jude Bellingham, and Rodri also in contention.

Presented by magazine France Football since 1956, it recognises the player judged the best in the world across the previous season. And since 2018, there has been the equivalent prize for the women’s game. Here’s all you need to know about tonight’s ceremony…

Kane is the bookies' favourite to win the Ballon d'Or tonight after scoring 73 goals in 65 games

With the exception of 2010-2015, when Zurich took the honour, the Ballon d’Or ceremony is held in France – with the last seven years in the Theatre du Chatelet in Paris. But this year the Palladium in London’s West End will host – perhaps a good omen for Kane. UEFA explained in May that the choice of venue is symbolic in tribute to inaugural winner Stanley Matthews, who received his award at Blackpool Town Hall. Seventy years on, and having accumulated a little more glitz and glamour over the decades, it returns to England.

Tonight’s show will be hosted by Arsenal and France legend Thierry Henry for the first time, alongside presenter Kate Scott, who returns for a fourth year. The 45-year-old is best known for her work on Champions League nights at US network CBS Sports, where she is a regular host alongside pundits Henry, Jamie Carragher and Micah Richards.

Henry – who was nominated for the award nine times – said: 'London is the city I call home, and I have a deep connection to its football culture and the passionate fans here, so to be hosting here alongside my friend and colleague Kate feels like a natural fit.’

Kane is looking to become the first English winner of the award since Michael Owen in 2001

For the men’s and women’s awards a shortlist of 30 players is decided by journalists from both France Football and L'Equipe newspaper. This shortlist is then ranked by journalists from around the world – 100 for the men’s, 50 for the women’s – with their rankings combined to decide the winner.

Jurors are instructed to consider these factors: individual performances and impressive character; collective performances and titles won; class and fair play.

Who is favourite for the main awards, then?

Harry Kane has the best chance of ending England’s 25-year wait for the men’s Ballon d’Or, with the Three Lions captain the bookies’ favourite to take home the award tonight. And for good reason. The 33-year-old enjoyed a staggering year, with his 73 goals in 65 matches bettered only by Lionel Messi’s 79 goals when he won the prize in 2012. After his starring role against Arsenal in the Champions League last week, Kane insisted he remains in the dark as to his fate tonight, a reality that is eating away at his wife, Katie. 'I try not to think about it. Obviously it is hard not to. There are always rumours flying around and you try to stay away from it,’ he said. ‘I think my wife would prefer to know and my family… it's very stressful for everyone.'

Kane faces stiff competition though, with Kylian Mbappe a close second among the bookmakers tonight. Mbappe dazzled at the World Cup this summer, where he became the all-time leading goalscorer in the competition’s history, with a total of 22. However, his lack of silverware – both domestic and international – this year could count against the Frenchman.

‘There are many people that speak about me and the Ballon d'Or. Of course I've made history in the most important competition. Maybe it's a good year for this but people can vote according to what they think,’ he said. ‘I always think I'm the best (player in the world) but everyone has their own opinion.’

‘I always think I'm the best (player in the world) but everyone has their own opinion,' Mbappe said of his own prospects of winning the award

Barcelona’s Lamine Yamal closely follows Mbappe and Kane in the odds. The 19-year-old has the best trophy collection of the top three contenders – having won the World Cup and LaLiga – but his subdued role in Spain’s triumph may count against the youngster. Yamal’s numbers are dwarfed by Kane’s, too. He recorded 25 goals and 20 assists across 57 appearances, although the Spaniard has argued that the award should go to football’s most entertaining star, not the most clinical one.

Like Mbappe, Yamal has been campaigning ahead of the ceremony as he bids to become the youngest-ever winner. ‘I think the Ballon d'Or should go to the best player in the world, full stop,' he said. 'Not to a player who has scored 80 goals, because if you score 80 goals, you get the top scorer award for that. Those are two completely different things that people do not understand.' Whether Yamal wins the prize tonight or not, he is certainly destined to take home the accolade at some point during what promises to be a special career.

Other candidates: Lionel Messi, Rodri, Erling Haaland, Khvicha Kvaratskhelia.

Meanwhile, Alexia Putellas is on course to end Aitana Bonmati’s three-year grip on the Ballon d’Or Feminin tonight. Barcelona midfielder Bonmati is out of the running after suffering a fractured fibula last November, keeping the superstar sidelined until May. She helped guide her side to an historic quadruple but her efforts were not enough to make the shortlist this year. With 24 goals and 13 assists, Bonmati’s former Barca team-mate Putellas – who this summer joined the London City Lionesses – led the way in the midfielder’s absence and is the favourite to be recognised for her exceptional year.

Other candidates: Ewa Pajor, Khadija Shaw, Pernille Harder.

Putellas is on course to end Aitana Bonmati’s three-year grip on the Ballon d’Or Feminin tonight

The men’s and women’s prize are the two most eye-catching on offer – PSG’s Ousmane Dembele won it last year for the first time and Barcelona’s Aitana Bonmati landed the women’s crown for the third year in a row.

But there are also 11 more awards in a celebration of European football: men’s and women’s Kopa Trophy, for players under 21; the Yashin Trophy, for best goalkeeper, Gerd Muller Trophy for best striker, and Johan Cruyff Trophy for best coach.

There are also men’s and women’s awards for Club of the Year – and a Socrates Award, first given out in 2022 and for a footballer’s humanitarian work.

Last year that went to the Xana Foundation, founded by PSG boss Luis Enrique to support families with children facing serious illnesses – the Enriques’ daughter Xana died in August 2019 after battling bone cancer.

This year will also see the addition of a new prize: the BOAT (Ballon d'Or of All Time). The organisers have been keen to stress that it is not an official award like the ones above but it has already caused plenty of debate online anyway.

Fans are being asked to log-in to the Ballon d'Or website to vote on which past winner they believe was the best. However, they can only pick from one of the ten players to have picked up the golden ball on at least two occasions.

And those who pay the €1 fee to cast their vote will earn a chance at tickets to next year's ceremony.

This year will also see the addition of a new prize: the BOAT (Ballon d'Or of All Time)

The ceremony will be free to watch on DAZN for UK viewers.

The Ballon d'Or's official website will also provide live updates.

His record with Bayern Munich has been astonishing, up there with the goalscoring feats of Messi and Ronaldo in their pomp. To hit 73 goals, including his England strikes, in a single season is the stuff of legend and he came desperately close, with Jude Bellingham, to leading his country to its first World Cup final for 60 years. His closest challenger, Lamine Yamal, won the World Cup with Spain and is a stellar talent but did not have a good enough tournament individually to dislodge Kane.

It has to be Kane. I am one of those that puts a lot of stock in winning the World Cup and Lamine Yamal, my runner up tonight, did that. But the goal totals are staggering, and for that reason it has to be Kane lifting the award tonight. Kane won his domestic league with Bayern Munich, won the Super Cup, reached the semi-finals of the Champions League, and finished third in the World Cup, becoming England's all-time leading World Cup scorer in the process. His total of 73 goals last season blows his competition out of the water. Yamal is an exceptional player and will win multiple Ballon d'Or awards in his career. This year it's got to be Kane, though.

Kane said: ‘I think my wife would prefer to know and my family. It's very stressful for everyone'

For me, it can only be Harry Kane after a year of such extraordinary delivery: those 61 goals in 51 appearances for Bayern Munich last season and the highest ever goal tally by an Englishman in a single Champions League campaign. That from a creator and initiator, who also builds so much in the deeper-lying areas. The circumstances and pressure under which you achieve must also be factored in and that’s what made his six goals at the World Cup something quite apart. Kylian Mbappe and Lamine Yamal feature in the Ballon d'Or but they pale by comparison.

It has to be Harry Kane. Averaged over a goal a game and had a brilliant World Cup. The best goalscorer on the planet. It’s indisputable. He’s already claimed the European Golden Shoe and football’s most prestigious individual award must follow.

The former Barcelona captain's legacy is already firmly secure with two past wins in this competition, and there's nothing getting in the way of her clinching a record-equalling third tonight to join her national team-mate Aitana Bonmati. Aggrieved NWSL fans will continue to deplore their lack of recognition at this ceremony, but the misalignment between the dates the award covers and the north Americans' season means that the focus remains on Europe, and Putellas its queen. She captained Barcelona to a continental quadruple in 2025-26, clinching the Liga F, Copa de la Reina, Spanish Super Cup and Champions League, and Bonmati's absence from the competition due to injury only makes the end result more clear-cut.

→ Baca artikel asli

#1484. Everton Defeated At Arsenal

Goals from Ezri Konsa and Gabriel consigned Everton to a 2-0 defeat at the home of reigning Premier League champions Arsenal on Saturday.

Konsa opened the scoring inside seven minutes in north London, prodding home from close range after Martin Odegaard's dangerous corner popped up and was clawed on to the crossbar by Jordan Pickford.

The Blues responded relatively well to that early setback, though, and fashioned chances of their own before the half-time interval – most notably through Tyrique George, whose vicious right-footed effort was deflected behind for a corner following bright play on the left flank.

Thierno Barry also went close as the opening period drew to a close, but his glancing first-time effort was well stopped by David Raya after an under-hit header back to the Arsenal goalkeeper.

The Gunners landed their second blow of the contest midway through the second half, with Gabriel heading home Ben White's cross at the far post.

Everton came again and went close to halving the deficit, but Kiernan Dewsbury-Hall's shot angled just wide from a cross by James Garner, who filled in at left-back after Vitalii Mykolenko was forced off with injury.

The result moves Arsenal joint top of the league table.

Next up for Everton is a Carabao Cup fourth-round clash with Newcastle United at Hill Dickinson Stadium on Thursday (7.45pm GMT kick-off).

Juventus visit Genoa at Marassi on Wednesday in the Serie A Round 9 played mid-week.

The ex-Tottenham and PSG striker has failed to justify his €50m price tag so far this season, scoring just three goals, including only one in Serie A, in 11 appearances.

Kolo Muani has started nine times and played the full 90 minutes six times in Serie A.

Spalletti will provide the latest team news at a pre-match press conference at 16:00 CET [15:00 GMT] later today.

#1480. Michael Carrick returns to Carrington as bosses decide his Man United future: Glum manager pictured arriving at training base while Sir Jim Ratcliffe and the Glazers hold do-or-die summit

Published: 11:36 GMT, 26 October 2026 | Updated: 14:57 GMT, 26 October 2026

Michael Carrick has arrived at Manchester United’s training ground amid increasing uncertainty over his future as head coach.

Carrick was pictured driving into Carrington on Monday afternoon following a day of crisis talks among club owners and senior executives over whether to sack him.

United’s fringe first-team players and Under-21s including Tyler Fletcher, Harry Amass and Shea Lacey have a behind-closed-doors friendly against Hull City which kicked off at 2pm, but it is unclear if that is the reason for Carrick’s presence on his day off.

Chief executive Omar Berrada and director of football Jason Wilcox drove into the training ground early on Monday morning. The majority of the players have a rest day after Sunday’s 3-0 defeat to Bournemouth at Old Trafford, although Bruno Fernandes, Patrick Dorgu and Manuel Ugarte came into Carrington for treatment.

Carrick dropped Fernandes against Bournemouth and left him on the bench following the captain’s criticism of the team’s tactics in last week’s Champions League defeat to Como.

Berrada and Wilcox are understood to have held conference calls with co-chairmen Joel and Avram Glazer, and minority owner Sir Jim Ratcliffe to decide Carrick’s fate and identify a successor if the 45-year-old is axed.

Michael Carrick arrived at Carrington to watch the under-21s - with his bosses deciding his fate

Omar Berrada (left) and Jason Wilcox (right) are seen arriving at Carrington ahead of crisis talks with Sir Jim Ratcliffe and the Glazers

Bruno Fernandes came into Carrington despite the players being on a day off

Manchester United's head honchos are locked in talks about Carrick's future

Eddie Howe and Julian Nagelsmann, both currently out of work and available to take over immediately, are the leading candidates after bookmakers stopped taking bets on Carrick being sacked.

The former United midfielder has 18 months left on the two-year contract he signed in May after guiding the club back into the Champions League.

After playing away in Spain and Italy over the last fortnight, United have a free week after going out of the Carabao Cup to Brighton and aren’t in action again until they meet Chelsea at Stamford Bridge on Saturday lunchtime.

Carrick’s job is hanging by a thread after United suffered a sixth defeat in 12 games this season against Bournemouth. Afterwards the visibly emotional head coach appeared to say farewell to the fans, although he later insisted that was not the case.

He also revealed that he had not spoken to Berrada or Wilcox over his future, although it’s understood that discussions have been taking place over the growing crisis among the United hierarchy before Sunday’s defeat.

‘Just briefly they came down like they always do after a game, but nothing out of the ordinary,’ said Carrick.

‘I totally understand the situation. I'm not expecting anything either way. I’m carrying on as normal. I love this place. I'll always do what I feel is going to be the best for the football club and I won’t change that now, whatever decision is made.

‘It's a tough situation, there's no hiding from it. What it looks like and what's next I really don't know. I love what I do and I feel the responsibility. We'll have to wait and see.

‘Of course, I take responsibility. I respect the position that I'm in, and the responsibility is mine. What else can I say really right now?

‘I've tried to do everything I can to not be in this position, of course I have. For a number of reasons, we've not been able to stop that happening to the position we're in right now.

‘I've been at the club a long time. It hurts an awful to see us going through this at this moment in time. The situation is where we are and we haven't had results or performances that have been good enough.

‘I'm feeling it for my players, they're going through a tough spell. There's a lot of emotion in the changing room right now, there's a lot of the boys quite hurt, to be fair.

‘It's not like they don't care or they want to be in this position. The ones I've spoken to, it’s affecting them a lot.’

#1477. A single archaeon couples long-chain fatty acid oxidation to methanogenesis

Thank you for visiting nature.com. You are using a browser version with limited support for CSS. To obtain the best experience, we recommend you use a more up to date browser (or turn off compatibility mode in Internet Explorer). In the meantime, to ensure continued support, we are displaying the site without styles and JavaScript.

Nature (2026) Cite this article

Anaerobic degradation of complex organic compounds to methane and carbon dioxide is common in the deep subsurface biosphere and has long been considered to require syntrophic cooperation, with methanogens confined to the terminal use of simple C1 and C2 substrates. Thermodynamic considerations, multi-omics analyses and microcosm experiments suggest that archaeal lineages are capable of methanogenic degradation of complex substrates1,2,3,4,5,6. However, experimental validation in pure culture was lacking. Here we report the isolation of a thermophilic archaeon, Methanoglobus dolicholipophagus AA-75, affiliated with Archaeoglobi, from a Chinese petroleum reservoir. Strain AA-75 not only converts methanol into methane, carbon dioxide and acetate, but also uses saturated fatty acids varying from C7:0 to C18:0 and unsaturated fatty acids including C16:1, C18:1 and C18:2 to produce methane. We term the latter lipotrophic methanogenesis, which integrates β-oxidation of fatty acids with the Wood–Ljungdahl pathway and the canonical methyl-tetrahydromethanopterin:coenzyme M methyltransferase and methyl-coenzyme M reductase complexes, together with an intracellular electron transfer system. A formaldehyde-methanol branch probably functions as an auxiliary route for C1 metabolism during methanogenesis. Methane produced from stearate is moderately 13C-enriched (δ13C–CH4 = −40.0‰ to −36.3‰), distinct from the strong 13C depletion characteristic of canonical methanogenic pathways. These findings demonstrate that methanogens can function as primary degraders of complex organic matter, challenging the classical syntrophic paradigm and calling for refinement of current models of carbon cycling in the deep subsurface biosphere.

#1475. Exclusive: From Pro Patria to the World – Estrella’s new model for running football clubs

This past July, Pro Patria became the first Italian club Estrella Football Group acquired: Football Italia sits down with Estrella’s co-founder and President, David Dwinger, to learn more about the group’s plans for the club and how the project reflects its wider vision for football.

Success in football can take many forms. It can mean lifting trophies on the pitch, building a sustainable club, or developing a thriving youth academy that nurtures the next generation of talent.

Coming from a family of Dutch champions and Olympians, David Dwinger understands the dedication and sacrifice required to compete at the highest level. Through Estrella Football Group, his goal is to create the environment and foundations that allow that potential to flourish.

In July, Estrella Football Group acquired Pro Patria, its third club after Portugal-based Cascais SAD and Carvoeiro United.

“We started off buying Cascais because it was just a childhood dream to have a football club,” Dwinger says in an exclusive interview with Football Italia.

Estrella Football Group is a European football investment company developing a horizontal multi-club ownership model.

Rather than imposing a rigid hierarchy, the model lets clubs remain independent while benefiting from shared knowledge, operational resources, and long-term planning. The ultimate mission is to create an environment where their clubs can compete at a higher level while strengthening their foundations for long-term growth.

With a strong background in sport tech companies, David and Estrella Group have been looking for the next standard, the next club, since they bought Cascais in 2024.

“In digital worlds everything is scalable. You just add more servers, and you can scale to the next 100,000 customers. In football, that’s different,” he says.

When the company announced it was looking to buy another club after Cascais, about 100 signed up, so Estrella started to set its own criteria: “We started looking at the country, the city, the catchment area and of course, the finance, a big part of it,” David says.

“Then the people who work there and the history. We effectively started building our first set of criteria, which I think now contains 45 criteria, and we built a tool.

“That’s how we started gathering information,” he continues.

“Currently, I think we have over 3,000 clubs in our tool. Predominantly from Europe. Predominantly from football countries. But Italy is a bigger football country than others, of course.”

The process starts long before formal negotiations. When a club makes contact, Estrella Football Group analyses a wide range of available data using its proprietary platform. A strong overall assessment opens the door to more detailed discussions about a possible investment.

So, what stood out about Pro Patria, an Italian Serie D club based in Busto Arsizio, in the region of Lombardy?

“I really liked the history as well as the close proximity to Milan. There’s, of course, a lot of football going on there,” David says.

“I liked the context, which is very difficult as a criterion. But it’s very important to us. So, the context, in this case, specifically of Luca Bassi, who is very important for us as a partner, because he’s effectively our equal partner.

“He is from the city. He’s very much emotionally attached to the club, and that brings a lot of value to us. We also see the potential there, and the infrastructure is quite good. The stadium may be a bit old, but there’s room to grow.

“It should be a Serie C club, of course. And we see the potential to bring it at least to Serie B. Probably not in the next two years. But it has that potential.”

The Estrella Football Group has four macro-areas that describe the types of clubs they could acquire.

There are ‘Brand clubs’, where the location makes the difference: “Beautiful places, like where I am today,” David says from his office in Cascais.

“Technically, this club can’t get beyond the fourth division in the current location. So, that’s a big issue. The reality is that I can’t go beyond a certain level, but it will never lose the location or the name.

“Then we look at academy clubs, which are very strong for their youth departments,” David continues.

“Then we look at performance clubs, which for us are the clubs which could have the potential to already be on a pro level, like Serie A, or Serie B.

“And then we look at fallen angels. They’re closely related to performance clubs, used to be big clubs, and have been struggling over the last few years.

“Pro Patria, for me, sits between a performance club and an academy club.”

And the academy focus should not be overlooked; quite the opposite. A key feature of Estrella Football Group, in fact, is the balance between collective support and local autonomy. On the one hand, clubs within the group share resources and best practices, but, locally, they continue to develop their own sporting project, keeping a strong connection with supporters and local communities.

“I feel that the academy is the base of a club. So, I think we need to have a strong academy with the potential to make these guys really become pro players,” David says.

“In the end, that’s what you dream of when you’re a little boy, right? You like to play football. You like to play with your mates, train, have fun, and enjoy it.

“My son used to play at the Ajax academy and for me, that’s the starting point. A club needs a strong academy to grow. Not every player will make it to the first team, but my ambition, and Estrella Football Club’s, should be to achieve the highest growth from the academy to the first team.

“I think our clubs should be known for giving out youth contracts,” he continues.

“I’d like to have it broader because I believe once we do that, it adds something to your club, to the infrastructure and, very importantly, to the scouting model. If you are known for that, then the scouting becomes inbound. So, we need to change that youth model, that academy model.”

Every city, every region, every country has its own story, but what challenges and opportunities does investing in Italian football present?

“Exceptions, of course, are everywhere, but I see a lot of Fallen Angels. It feels like a high percentage of clubs in Italy are Fallen Angels, maybe more than in the Netherlands or Spain. It feels like it, maybe because we look at Italy a lot.

“What I would really like to ensure is that clubs like Pro Patria, especially now that we’re involved, become really stable and not opportunistic.

“There’s a lot of culture, a lot of emotion. So we should build it up even more, right? Imagine showing movies on the pitch, like an open-air cinema, and that movie night also gives you a free ticket to the next match. Or organising gala dinners at the stadium.

“And we can build content around that, and that content allows us to attract more eyeballs and get people to buy shirts in countries which are maybe even close to Italy. It’s exactly what’s happening all year in Portugal. I truly feel we can do that because Pro Patria isn’t coming from nothing, right?

“We need to win everything, sure. But then there’s also realism and building a system that is sustainable, as self-sustainable as possible.”

The project is about building a solid network and sharing expertise and opportunities, which will lead to success on the field. It’s not about millions of euros in new signings.

“I want to build a system and a foundation which works to ensure that the club will be here for 100 more years. When we focus on the business side, it positively affects what’s happening on the field.

“I want to be promoted, but not just for the sake of promotion. I want promotion because we’ve done everything well. And that should result in promotion.

“And that the business side and business intelligence and structures and frameworks all complement the drive to win. And actually, we’ve seen that work a couple of times now.

“I want to win. We’re all entrepreneurs as well, so I’m always surrounded by people who want to win, but we want to work directly with the club’s local teams, because that’s where the local knowledge sits, the cultural understanding.

“Working with [Pro Patria President] Rosanna [Zema] and [Sporting director] Giovanni [Giovanditti], and then the new head of youth, Augusto [Splendore], and Luca [Bassi], who’s based in London.

“We want to work with the local teams. We do that for all the clubs. And then we implement our tooling.”

Each club works locally, but how do they share information and knowledge and, ultimately, help each other grow?

“We’ve built an application called ClubOS. It’s an operating system for a football club,” David says.

“It sits in every department, which we implement at all our clubs. External clients also use it. We’ve got a couple of pro clubs in the Netherlands using that tool, and we’re now rolling it out for Europe.

“We’ve got a lot of ideas,” David continues.

“We can do Estrella tournaments. The logical part is player training, player pathways, youth. But also from the staff perspective.

“For example, two years ago, the staff of Cascais went to Atletico Madrid for two days to work with their technical staff. And of course, Atletico is not an Estrella club, but it is a client of one of our applications. So that ecosystem, that network, is what we’re always trying to optimise and implement.

“There’s a lot of knowledge per football club, which is within the walls of a football club and doesn’t really get out. We want to optimise that knowledge within our clubs, not just dictate from our side.

“We have strong opinions on how to run a football club, but we miss the local details. So this is how we work together.

“I think it will be great to start setting up Estrella events where all the staff come together. One year we go to Pro Patria, the next year we go to a club in Portugal, and now we’re working on a club in Austria and the UK. So that’s all very specific knowledge with a lot of people with a lot of experience.”

There are already projects taking shape, involving Pro Patria as well.

“These are very early conversations. But we are looking at the pitch at Pro Patria, which could use improvement. We are fairly closely connected to a very large Dutch company, TenCate, which creates pitches.

“They actually have a plant and a factory in Italy, so we’re now in conversations with them on maybe starting a partnership with Estrella, so that all the Estrella clubs can get a great pitch from them.

“It’s the same we’ve done with the ticketing system, or Catapult, the GPS system. These are all partners for Estrella.”

A fairly small team runs all of Estrella’s management. The daily work involves David’s two ventures, founding partner Alessandro Nuccetelli, Estrella co-founder and CEO Xander Czaikowski, former CEO of PEC Zwolle in the Netherlands, and Global Sports Director Cristiano Piccini, a former Italy international and Valencia defender.

“We deliberately want to keep Estrella core team as small as possible. Because every euro I spend on a new guy is not going towards buying a football club,” David concludes.

“Estrella’s goal is to buy five clubs per year. We believe that with our horizontal MCO, we’re the world’s first horizontal MCO, and that if we put the clubs next to each other, rather than stacking them on top of each other, this one becomes less important than the top one. We want to do it like this. That means that with every club we add, we add something else to the flywheel.”

Lorenzo Bettoni is the Editor of Football Italia.

#1474. Carrick's gamble on Bruno may prove his last act as Man United boss but his message is clear: the lunatics have run the asylum for far too long: KIERAN GILL on My Premier League Weekend

Published: 12:00 GMT, 26 October 2026 | Updated: 15:05 GMT, 26 October 2026

It carried shades of Ruud Gullit dropping Alan Shearer for Newcastle United against Sunderland in August 1999. Newcastle led 1-0 at half-time. Then Shearer was introduced at 1-1. Then Sunderland won 2-1. Gullit departed the club after that Tyne–Wear derby defeat at St James’ Park.

Michael Carrick may not last much longer at Manchester United either now that he has produced his own famous axeing. One difference between Shearer and Bruno Fernandes is that the latter did not appear at all in their 3-0 loss to Bournemouth at Old Trafford - an unused substitute to the end.

United have Chelsea next, and those at Stamford Bridge are used to facing a United caretaker in his first league game in charge. Carrick in 2021: a 1-1 draw. Ruud van Nistelrooy in 2024: another 1-1 draw. Darren Fletcher in 2026? Quite possibly. All that remains is the picture of the corner flag popping up on United’s social media feed to confirm Carrick’s departure.

Yet in what was potentially his last act as United manager, Carrick took a stand. He refused to placate Fernandes, who is a fabulous footballer, if also the master of moaning. His ego may be dented for being made an example of, but in truth, this was about more than the Portuguese and his hissy fits.

His disobedience as club captain afforded Carrick the opportunity to send the message that the lunatics have been running the asylum for too long. It is a team that have chewed and spewed too many managers.

Fernandes publicly criticised his coaches’ tactics after he was substituted during their 4-1 Champions League loss at Como. He could have been dropped for poor performances sooner, but his outburst forced the issue, and he was deservedly punished for his midweek act of mutiny.

It was a risk by Carrick, and one which did not deliver him the win he would have hoped for. He is not the first, nor will he be the last, manager to potentially use his dying breath to send such a message, however.

Bruno Fernandes's axeing could prove Michael Carrick's final act... 27 years after Ruud Gullit left Newcastle after leaving Alan Shearer on the bench against Sunderland

Michael Carrick acknowledges United fans at Old Trafford after the final whistle - but insisted it was not a wave goodbye and he does not know whether he will keep his job

One of the candidates to replace him, Antonio Conte, did so at Tottenham Hotspur. His speech after their 3-3 draw at Southampton was stunning. He essentially called on the club to end its excuse culture. He went down swinging. Conte was gone a week later.

So Carrick took his own swing. He may have missed, what with losing 3-0 without Fernandes, but it was needed as a reminder to United’s players that they are not bigger than their club.

And for that, good on Carrick, who may now lose his job as a consequence. At least he will have gone out with the supporters at Old Trafford singing his name before they showed the players what they thought of them.

But if United fans want to feel a little better, Newcastle replaced Gullit with the wonderful Sir Bobby Robson, and Shearer finished that same season scoring 30 goals.

You can think Mikel Arteta is slightly strange, but in this world of managers giving mundane answers to the media we should be thankful that every so often, the man in charge of the champions is willing to provide us with new insight.

This time, after being booked during the 2-0 win over Everton, Arteta told us how he has been clipping up footage of other head coaches stepping out of their technical area during matches. He insists he has evidence that another manager did so more than 30 times in 10 minutes without receiving a yellow.

That was gold dust for the World Wide Web. A glance at one online forum threw up a ton of ideas. Set up sensors around the technical area and have managers wear shock collars! Give the fourth official a water spray to teach them a lesson like a misbehaving cat! Set up rakes around the white lines so when they step on them, they get hit like Sideshow Bob from The Simpsons!

Good suggestions, all. It would certainly give Howard Webb’s Pro Ref one fewer issue to think about when they should have bigger concerns while trying to officiate the Premier League fairly.

Mikel Arteta clashes with fourth official Ruebyn Ricardo at the Emirates on Saturday after stepping out of his technical area

Jokes aside, from an Arsenal perspective, it is good that Arteta is willing to fight the narrative that he is the only one who behaves badly in his technical area. He is not. We all know he is not, and we should believe him when he claims he can prove it to Webb and Co.

Within that dossier might now be a clip of Roberto De Zerbi celebrating Tottenham scoring by invading the pitch at Chelsea. He had already been booked for a bust-up with the opposition bench.

Had this been Arteta, we might have heard more talk of what a naughty boy he is, and how he gets away with too much. But Arsenal should be glad that their manager is happy to hit back.

United lost, so inevitably, the temptation is to purely discuss them and their troubles. But it would be wrong for us to disregard the role played by Bournemouth, who secured their fourth consecutive win without conceding despite juggling European football for the first time ever.

Thursday: 3-0 win over Sturm Graz, Europa League. Sunday: 2-0 win over Sunderland, Premier League. Thursday: 3-0 win over AC Milan, Europa League. Sunday: 3-0 win over United, Premier League. Their fixture list before the November international break has Marco Rose’s team playing Wednesday, Saturday, Thursday, Sunday in three different competitions.

Which is one hell of a slog for a side not used to all this, and the greatest challenge they face. The travelling. The recovery. The training sessions they then squeeze in. Over the last two weeks, Bournemouth have handled it with impressive ease.

If they can continue to handle this workload, it can only help the club convince their players that they should not be seen as a stepping stone.

That is what Bournemouth, led by their owner, Bill Foley, want.

They do not want to be a temporary home to the Antoine Semenyos and Illia Zabarnyis and Dean Huijsens who find their way to them. They want to get to a place where they keep them.

Chelsea found out the day before playing Tottenham that they would not have Joao Pedro, whose injury is being assessed and with no timeline yet placed on his return.

Xabi Alonso could have simply added Danny Welbeck to his starting line-up as a like-for-like replacement, a striker for a striker. But he tried something different.

He added Geovany Quenda to his line-up. It was his first start for Chelsea, and he acted as another No 10 alongside Cole Palmer while Morgan Rogers was a false nine.

With the power of hindsight, we can say it smacked of Alonso overthinking the problem as Chelsea never looked like beating Spurs with their 11th-hour shake-up. That the tactics then resembled ‘hoof and hope’ only made it more confusing that an actual striker was not used.

As for Tottenham, that first victory at Stamford Bridge since 2018 should be a turning point for De Zerbi, more so than their win at Coventry which was helped by Carl Rushworth.

The performance itself was brimming with positivity, even as they impressively coped while losing James Maddison to a second-half injury. His replacement, Conor Gallagher, was like bait for Chelsea as a former player. Palmer fouled him to receive his second yellow. Rogers was fortunate that the refereeing team did not think he deserved red for giving Gallagher a kicking of his own.

Conor Gallagher was a nuisance against his former club and Morgan Rogers was lucky to avoid a red card for kicking him

The man may have taken over a Crystal Palace team which won the Conference League last season, but he inherited problems, and the suits upstairs cannot escape scrutiny for the players now at his disposal. They practically sold their back line without properly replacing them, and now have the worst defensive record in the Premier League.

Liam Delap does not help his image. He wants to be known as a footballer, but can sometimes bring to mind that bloke in Sunday league who is more interested in scrapping.

On Friday, the Nottingham Forest striker went for the ball with Ipswich captain Dara O’Shea, who was left with a shirt ripped to smithereens. Chelsea tired of that side to Delap’s game, and his lack of scoring, and did extraordinarily well to secure £50million for him in the summer.

Liam Delap tangles with Ipswich's Dara O'Shea during the 1-1 draw at Portman Road on Friday

A text from a Craven Cottage contact a couple of weeks ago told us how even some players inside the Fulham changing room were unsure of Alvaro Arbeloa’s methods.

Arbeloa appeared on the brink at that time, then Fulham won twice in the Premier League, beating Hull City 1-0 and Coventry City 1-0.

Yet two narrow victories against newly-promoted sides will not end the noise entirely. Fulham’s next four league fixtures are against Aston Villa, Newcastle, Manchester City, Bournemouth. That is where he will be judged much more seriously.

Mark Chapman is up there with the best sports broadcasters in the country, and normally such enjoyable company in our living rooms. We have to hope 'Chappers' is doing OK because his subdued appearance on Match of the Day – the BBC show beleaguered of late – was concerning to watch.
