#1471. Allegri: ‘Given up on the appeal, might end up with a four-game ban instead of two’

Massimiliano Allegri has confirmed that he has ‘given up’ on his appeal of the two-match suspension that he was given after Napoli’s recent 3-3 draw against Roma, and has identified where his side need to improve heading into Wednesday evening’s game against Monza in Serie A.

Allegri sat down for his customary pre-match press conference ahead of Napoli’s upcoming Serie A match against Monza, which kicks off at 19.45 GMT on Wednesday evening.

During his talk with the press, Allegri discussed a number of topics including his touchline outburst over the weekend, which has earned him a two-match suspension, his side’s need to improve defensively and the fitness of Scott McTominay, Andre-Frank Zambo Anguissa and Antonio Vergara.

The first questions that came Allegri’s way referred to his red card at the end of Napoli 3-3 Roma and the fiery comments that Allegri was reported to have made in the tunnel.

“There’s not much more to say, so much has happened now. This morning, I decided to give up on the appeal. You never know, I might end up with a four-game ban instead of two,” Allegri joked.

“The biggest regret isn’t the suspension, but the fact that we didn’t bring home the three points after playing a good game. The rest is all talk. It’s a case of would have/should have. Not what he did say, but what he might’ve said. Let’s have a laugh about it now.

“I’m sorry I won’t be there in the dugout. I enjoy going to Turin. Those who will be there will do a better job than me, I have a great staff.”

When Gian Piero Gasperini spoke at a press conference on Monday, he said that the way the incident unfolded and the way it was reported was ‘unfortunate’.

Asked for a response, Allegri said: “I didn’t hear what he said, but the words that were reported might’ve been wrong. But we can’t change the result of the suspension now. We have to work hard, we’re behind in the standings and all we can do is work hard to try and improve it, to improve on certain in-game situations where we’re letting ourselves down at the moment.

“Maybe in a month, things will have changed, but we have to work on the performances. The players’ conditions are improving. Several have been out for two months like McTominay, Anguissa; Alisson has been in and out, Neres has been injured, now Badiashile. We have to get those who are out back in and improve the condition of those guys who have played less.”

Allegri began to dissect his side’s recent 3-3 draw against Roma and the lessons that he has learned from that outing.

“We had a good game against Roma. We need to do better in terms of distribution. At times, we forced it against Roma when we could have managed possession better. At 2-0, we should have controlled it better. In the first halves, we have only conceded two goals this season. In the second halves, we’ve conceded eight, so there’s data that shows us where to improve and that helps us understand that we need to be better in certain moments.”

Allegri was asked about his recent tactical tweaks and whether this has been the reason behind Napoli’s improved performances over the last week.

He said: “If Lobotka plays in front of the defence, he has the right characteristics for it, he’s an excellent player. If Gilmour plays there, it’s different, with De Bruyne, it’s more direct, he can change the game in an instant. It’s a matter of characteristics. They give you different ways of playing. De Bruyne is extraordinary at playing these 50-yard passes, but he can’t play every single game like some of the others, so changes become fundamental.”

And what about Andre-Frank Zambo Anguissa, who played the full 90 minutes against Roma over the weekend, but might need his minutes managing after recent injury issues.

“The only way to get them back to their best condition is to let them play,” Allegri insisted. “He’s been rested since last year, he’s not played enough.”

There were also updates on Amir Rrahmani and Antonio Vergara: “Rrahmani has returned to the group, but he’s been out for many months. We need to be good but also a little lucky to get results and rehabilitate players at the same time.”

And on Vergara, he added: “He’s available, Antonio has excellent qualities. He’s a winger who loves to come inside, a raider, he likes to get into the box. He’s got seven or eight goals in him in a league season. He’s got his own characteristics, he has to improve in some areas of the game in terms of tactics and individual technique.”

And how about Rasmus Hojlund, who has put in some selfless performances of late but is without a goal himself for the last eight games at club level. Is there a chance that Lorenzo Lucca replaces him at centre-forward?

“Lucca will definitely have an opportunity to play, he had an excellent pre-season, he’s scored goals. He might feel the pressure a bit more, but he’s given us his availability. Against Genoa, he came on and provided the action that led to the goal. We have to be confident of his qualities.

“Hojlund is very strong physically and now he’s taking games to the very end, not falling short in the 60th minute. He had three big chances the other day, but did well.”

Lastly, Allegri was asked about his side’s defensive output. Despite a win and a draw over their last two matches, Napoli have conceded six in those games and have shipped 10 in their last four games, letting in three goals in three of those games.

Allegri said: “I’ve seen some data. We’re conceding an average of four shots on target per game, but that doesn’t tell you how we concede them or where we’re conceding them from.

“The data says we concede four shots on target, so we have to work on ourselves when we are defending our own area, like for Soule’s first goal or the third goal against Roma. We have to work on that, there will be some mistakes, we will be better at other times.”

Peter Young is a Senior Reporter with Football Italia.

#1468. Soccer's woke mob attacked me before I was forcibly removed from a women's game... all because of my T-shirt. This is my story about the shocking viral ordeal

Published: 15:36 GMT, 26 October 2026 | Updated: 15:50 GMT, 26 October 2026

A conservative podcaster has spoken out after being allegedly assaulted, whipped in the face with flagpoles and forcibly hauled out of a professional women's soccer match for wearing clothing supporting single-sex sports.

Brandi Kruse, host of the 'unDivided' podcast, was physically removed by security guards from Providence Park on Friday night during the Portland Thorns' clash against the North Carolina Courage.

Kruse initially took to social media to showcase her ejection, while also highlighting the abuse that she and her group faced due to their presence inside the stadium.

Her footage captured the tense confrontations with fans, one group member having beer thrown at them and stadium security demanding she leave the stadium over her XX-XY apparel. XX-XY Athletics opposes transgender athlete inclusion in women's sports and was founded by author and retired artistic gymnast Jennifer Sey.

Speaking in an exclusive interview with The Daily Mail, Kruse described the terrifying scenes of mob hostility inside the stadium stands.

According to Kruse, the group kept their jackets fully zipped to conceal their apparel until the opening whistle of the second half.

Podcast host Brandi Kruse was physically removed by security guards from Providence Park

Kruse and her group were ejected after brandishing their XX-XY t-shirts in the stands

A number of fans were seen hurling abuse towards the group before they were ejected

'We actually think that they suspected something around the end of the first half because we saw that the head sort of cheerleader for the supporter club - a woman who was wearing a 'Protect Trans Kids' shirt with a knife on it - she recognized someone in the group,' Kruse revealed.

'By the time the whistle blew for the start of the second half, we were surrounded by people who had trans pride flags ready,' Kruse said.

'As soon as we took our jackets off, then they started to wave the flags, which honestly is fine. It's a fan section; there's always flags waving. But then that turned into screaming and violence within moments.'

Kruse detailed how fans concealed physical strikes behind the oversized banners, lashing out at the demonstrators and working media.

'Well, I think the most severe assault that occurred was a 57-year-old woman with our group,' Kruse explained. 'She's a lesbian, a longtime Democrat, which I think makes this all the crazier. She was pushed over two rows of seating by a male Thorns fan, and she was pretty shaken up after that.

'But you know what they do, and I see this often with sort of left-wing riots and things, is they'll wave these flags or signs and pretend to accidentally hit you with them - like, 'Oh, oops' - and they'll just keep doing it. And so, we're basically just being whipped with trans pride flags in the face repeatedly.

'There was also a reporter there for OutKick who was standing in a pretty neutral area just recording when his phone was knocked out of his hand. He bent down to pick it up, and they poured beer all over him.

'So, yeah. I would say I didn't walk away with any injuries besides my wrist, which was an injury inflicted by a security guard. But from the fans, it was mostly pushing, hitting with the end of sticks, and whipping us in the face with flags.'

Kruse shared a detailed account of the group's gameday experience with The Daily Mail

One fan in Kruse's videos was seen holding up a middle finger to the group on the day

Kruse revealed that a number of fans had banners and scarves displaying political messages

Pictured: Julie, the 57-year-old woman who was thrown down two rows of seating on Friday

Kruse identified the 57-year-old victim as a woman named Julie, confirming that a police report has been lodged.

'It was caught on video from multiple angles, and she did file a police report right after the game with the Portland Police Department,' Kruse revealed.

'Actually, she talked to the man after a couple minutes had gone by, and he apologized. He said he didn't even know why he pushed her or what people were mad at us about; he was just sort of following the mob of the crowd.'

According to FOX, the Portland Police Bureau have since confirmed that 'two potential crimes' took place on Friday night and that an investigation is ongoing.

Meanwhile, rather than intervening to stop the hostile crowd, venue security cracked down entirely on the demonstrators wearing the shirts.

'I expected a bad reaction from the fans; I did not expect for us to be told we had to leave, and not the individuals who were actively blocking our view, assaulting us, and screaming at us,' Kruse continued.

'They would not tell us anything. A lot of the people we were with were kind of elderly, and so when they were told, 'You need to leave,' they just got up and left.

'I decided, 'I'm not going to leave. If they want me to leave, they're going to have to own that decision.' I said, 'I'm not leaving of my own free will.' And that's when two of them grabbed me by both arms.'

Kruse detailed the physical force used to escort her through the concourse: 'The female security guard put my arm behind my back and twisted it, which surprised me with the level of force. I had a conversation with her; it's all on camera.

The conservative podcast host seen getting escorted out of Providence Park by security

Multiple security guards were seen refraining Kruse as she exited the Portland stadium

Kruse detailed the physical force used to escort her: 'The female security guard put my arm behind my back and twisted it, which surprised me with the level of force'

'I said, 'Look, I haven't done anything wrong. I'm wearing a T-shirt. We're the ones being assaulted. Can you just explain to me why we're being asked to leave, but they're not being asked to leave?''

'And she says, 'We're not having that conversation down here. We can have the conversation up there' - which they never did. They threw us out onto the streets of Portland without any explanation. We never got one.

'Initially, a male security guard had come down, and he cleared the second row... I had asked him, 'Why are we being removed?' And he said, "Well, we'll talk about that."

'Then he said, 'Well, you're standing on a chair,' as if that was sort of a reason he threw out,' Kruse recalled.

'But I had stood up on a chair to get away from a scrum that was happening and the people blocking our view and hitting us. I'd gotten on the chair to escape the violence of the moment!'

Reflecting on the harrowing ordeal, Kruse warned that the nation is now locked in a dangerous struggle against ideological extremism.

'I'm almost 40 years old, thinking, 'What are you doing, Brandi?' But it's gone beyond the issue of girls' sports. It's Stalinism,' Kruse explained.

'These people are willing to use any means necessary to silence dissenting voices, and I think that's a very scary thing for America.'

Another Portland Thorns fan was seen holding middle fingers up to Kruse and her group

Pictured: The security guard who led Kruse out of Providence Park during the game

'I think that the angrier the crowds get and the more authoritarian they act when it comes to silencing dissent, the more I feel like I have to stay involved in the fight.

'You don't have to agree with our cause of protecting women's sports to understand how dangerous that is in the United States.'

The ejection has sparked immediate federal scrutiny, with the US Department of Justice confirming on Saturday that civil rights officials have stepped in.

'The Justice Department is aware of these reports and will review the matter thoroughly,' a DOJ spokesperson told Fox News.

'Based on what is known so far, the conduct described appears to raise viewpoint discrimination and First Amendment questions rather than sex discrimination.'

Reacting to the DOJ intervention, Kruse argued that publicly owned sports venues cannot selectively enforce rules based on political viewpoints.

'I think that there's definitely viewpoint discrimination. I do wonder if, as a country, we need to start looking at politics as sort of a protected class,' Kruse explained.

'Providence Park is owned by the City of Portland - it's owned by the taxpayer - and so that makes all of this all the more complicated.'

A DOJ spokesperson said 'the conduct described appears to raise viewpoint discrimination and First Amendment questions'

'If you don't want any political slogans at your games, I'm all for that. Make that a clear rule that's evenly enforced, and then people can enjoy the games absent politics.

'But if you're going to allow political slogans from one side, then you have to allow it from the other, or you're going to find yourself in the middle of these situations time and time again.'

The discrimination reportedly followed the demonstrators into the night, with Kruse sharing that a ride-share driver refused to take group members once their shirts were spotted, leaving a disabled woman stranded on a dark city street.

Friday's demonstration was organized by women's rights campaigner Amy Sousa, who was ejected from a Thorns match last month without explanation after wearing a shirt featuring a small XX-XY logo.

The Portland Thorns have not responded to requests for comment.

#1466. Official: Allegri confirms 20-man Napoli squad for Monza clash with two absences

Massimiliano Allegri has named a squad of 20 players for Napoli’s upcoming Serie A match against Monza on Wednesday evening, welcoming Amir Rrahmani back to the pack but having to go without Benoit Badiashile and Leonardo Spinazzola.

A full round of midweek Serie A fixtures is taking place over the next three nights, beginning with Sassuolo vs. Lazio at 17.30 GMT on Tuesday and ending with Fiorentina vs. Atalanta at 19.45 GMT on Thursday. Napoli will take on Monza away from home at 19.45 GMT on Wednesday night.

Napoli are coming in off the back of an eventful 3-3 draw against Roma over the weekend, which saw head coach Allegri sent off in the final minutes after his explosive reaction to Leonardo Balerdi’s late equaliser being deemed legal without the need for the referee to check it on the monitor.

Allegri has been given a two-match suspension as a result of his actions, and though the club announced that they would appeal the decision on Monday, the coach himself confirmed during his pre-match press conference on Tuesday that they are dropping the appeal.

Napoli also confirmed on Monday that Rrahmani has returned to full training with the first-team group, but that summer signing Badiashile is facing around a month out with a rectus femoris injury and that Leonardo Spinazzola will miss the Monza game with a minor thigh issue.

It is a case of one in, two out, as Rrahmani returns to the matchday squad while Badiashile and Spinazzola drop out.

Goalkeepers: Nikita Contini, Alex Meret, Vanja Milinkovic-Savic.

Defenders: Sam Beukema, Giovanni Di Lorenzo, Luca Marianucci, Rafa Marin, Mathias Olivera, Amir Rrahmani.

Midfielders: Andre-Frank Zambo Anguissa, Kevin De Bruyne, Billy Gilmour, Stanislav Lobotka, Scott McTominay.

Forwards: Giovane, Rasmus Hojlund, Noa Lang, Lorenzo Lucca, Matteo Politano, Antonio Vergara.

#1462. Blues' Goodison Streak Ended By Villa

Everton’s five-game winning streak at Goodison Park came to its conclusion as the Blues were defeated 3-2 by Aston Villa on Sunday afternoon.

Noelle Maritz broke the deadlock with just three minutes on the clock, with goals either side of half-time from Amalie Vangsgaard completing the scoring for the visitors.

Holly McNamara opened her account for the Club after signing in the summer on 79 minutes – the Aussie's arrowed strike from inside the area beating Akane Okuma.

Fellow summer recruit Noemie Mouchon then halved the deficit with 90 minutes on the clock, but Scott Phelan's side were left to feel the unfamiliar emotions of a home defeat.

Courtney Brosnan donned the captain’s armband and was trusted in between the sticks, with a backline of Maz Pacheco, Hannah Silcock, Naomi Layzell and Hannah Blundell in front of her.

Ruby Mace and Aurora Galli sat deep in midfield, allowing Yuka Momiki to roam slightly higher up. Hannah Cain and Ornella Vignola lined the flanks, with McNamara leading the line.

It was the visitors who broke the deadlock as Maritz' effort fortunately rebounded off the near-post inside four minutes.

At the other end, Cain threatened as the Blues searched for an immediate response. Akane Okuma was equal to her low effort, though.

Vangsgaard doubled the Villains' lead, however, as she lobbed Brosnan with an audacious attempt from 30-yards. Tracked by the retreating Mace, the Dane cut-inside and floated the ball over the head of the outrushed keeper in the 32nd minute.

That concluded the action for the opening 45, with Vangsgaard doubling her tally five minutes after the interval. The 29-year-old burst across the front-post to meet Maritz' ball – the defender turning provider after notching the first.

Phelan looked to his bench for a reaction and brought on Jutta Rantala and Clare Wheeler in place of Cain and Galli.

Mouchon and Rion Ishikawa then replaced Vignola and Silcock as the Blues went on the offence.

That soon paid off as McNamara's strike nestled in to the bottom left corner. Compatriot Wheeler managed to squeeze the ball to the feet of her Everton and Australia teammate, who arrowed home with her first touch to give the Evertonians hope with 11 minutes remaining.

Mouchon's cross then evaded the reach of Okuma with 90 minutes on the clock. From a wide position, the France-youth international lifted the ball over the shot-stopper – the Gwladys erupting with hopes of completing the comeback.

Brian Sorensen's side defended resolutely throughout the dying moments, though – the former-Blues head coach putting a stop to Everton's scorching streak at the Grand Old Lady.

Barcelona are reportedly interested in signing Tottenham Hotspur midfielder Lucas Bergvall as the Spanish giants look to strengthen their midfield options ahead of the January transfer window.

According to an exclusive report from TEAMtalk, the Catalan club has renewed its interest in the Swedish international, whose limited playing time under Roberto De Zerbi has drawn attention from several European heavyweights.

Although Barcelona already have talented young players such as Pedri, Gavi and Marc Bernal, Bergvall’s potential could make him a valuable long-term investment.

Tottenham remain in a strong negotiating position after persuading Bergvall to sign a new contract until 2031 last month. The north London club are reluctant to let him leave, particularly given their belief in his long-term potential.

Nevertheless, his lack of regular opportunities could become a concern if the situation continues over the coming months.

Barcelona may therefore look to exploit any uncertainty around his role at Spurs, although convincing the Premier League club to negotiate a deal will be difficult.

Much will depend on Bergvall’s playing time during the remainder of the campaign. If he continues to struggle for starts, interest from Europe’s biggest clubs could intensify, potentially setting up a significant transfer battle in 2027.

#1460. Forget Eddie Howe or Julian Nagelsmann. Man United need a boss with clout, class, gravitas and trophies... and that's Carlo Ancelotti, the Godfather of management

By IAN HERBERT, DEPUTY CHIEF SPORTS WRITER

Published: 17:00 GMT, 26 October 2026 | Updated: 17:48 GMT, 26 October 2026

Well intentioned though they might have been, there was a deadening familiarity about Harry Maguire’s soundbites after Sunday’s latest Old Trafford catastrophe, amplifying a sense of Manchester United being stuck in a godforsaken failure loop.

Maguire’s ‘look in the mirror’ and ‘take accountability’ and ‘wearing this shirt’ were the kind of words he and Luke Shaw have been trotting out for years. It’s white noise, now. None of this stuff makes the blindest difference.

But it was by the law of unintended consequences that Maguire revealed how Michael Carrick, a nice guy in a fix, simply doesn’t exude the authority required to command United. ‘Michael turned our season on its head last season,’ the defender observed after the 3-0 defeat by Bournemouth.

Never, in the days when United were a force in football, would the manager be described as ‘Alex’ or, decades earlier, ‘Matt’. Sir Alex Ferguson and Sir Matt Busby simply wouldn’t tolerate that kind of familiarity.

Judging by the names emerging as possible next inheritors of this most poisoned chalice – Eddie Howe and Julian Nagelsmann – there is no appreciation that monumental authority, self-assurance and powers of persuasion are required here, and that this is not just another turn in the annual game of managerial swings and roundabouts.

United require a manager capable of instilling calm in the madhouse, standards where they are missing, and fashioning an environment in which no one would dare contemplate acts of vicious mutiny from the likes of Bruno Fernandes. The less widely appreciated point is that United require a manager capable of inspiring the kind of players the club need to join this ship.

The words of Harry Maguire (above) in support of Michael Carrick gave away a familiarity between players and manager that Sir Alex Ferguson, for example, would never have tolerated

The next manager must be able to dazzle and impress targets as well as have authority within the Old Trafford dressing room. Carlo Ancelotti, a stellar competitor, fits the bill

United have cut player salaries hard within an extremely constricted budget under Sir Jim Ratcliffe and Ineos and the name of Manchester United, starved of the biggest trophies for so long domestically and on the European stage, no longer carries the old cachet.

The next manager must be able to dazzle and impress targets. With the greatest respect, Howe and Nagelsmann just won't do.

There has been a sense for weeks now that Carlo Ancelotti is the individual United have needed all along. A stellar competitor who has lifted five Champions League trophies and league titles in five countries, but who has ridden the rollercoaster for long enough to brush off the kind of vicissitudes which have destroyed so many at United.

He does not emit a lot of visible heat or light. There’s little of the rage from him in the technical area we see in Jose Mourinho, whose Real Madrid side’s victory in Sunday’s El Clasico led some to suggest he still has what United need. But the most combustible players certainly know who’s boss when Ancelotti's around.

Zlatan Ibrahimovic tells of the ‘Ancelotti eyebrow’, which put in an appearance on a bitterly cold night at Evian, during the player and coach’s time together at Paris Saint-Germain. As Ibrahimovic tells it, the pitch was terrible and PSG were not playing well.

‘At half-time, you always know when something is going on because that one eyebrow goes up. As I sat down I thought, “Now he’s angry”.

'There was a box in front of him and from nowhere he kicked it and it flew through the air and hit me on the head. “Jesus,” I thought. “Now he’s really angry”.’

Ibrahimovic adored Ancelotti. So did David Beckham, another who knows, from their time together at PSG, that the Italian can explode at times. ‘Scary,’ he called it.

Ancelotti with Zlatan Ibrahimovic in their PSG days. The Swedish striker was full of admiration for his manager

Does Eddie Howe really have the charisma or clout of five-time Champions League winner Ancelotti?

A book discussing the Italian's methods, which Ancelotti co-wrote with Mike Forde, his former sporting director at Chelsea, was entitled Quiet Leadership and in his preface he referenced Vito Corleone and The Godfather.

‘When you watch Corleone, do you see a weak quiet man?’ he wrote. ‘Or do you see a calm powerful man, in charge of his situation?

'My approach is born of the idea that a leader should not need to rant and rave or rule with an iron fist, but rather that their power should be implicit. It should be crystal clear who is in charge, and their authority must result from respect and trust, rather than fear.’

At Chelsea, he flourished in the face of bedlam. Roman Abramovich, the owner, suggested Ancelotti favoured English players, demanded he be ‘tougher’ with the dressing room and, on one occasion, summoned the manager to his London apartment for a 'dressing down’ after a 6-0 victory against West Bromwich Albion. He shrugged all this off.

When Abramovich demanded that Ancelotti ‘find a playing identity for the team’ he put Michael Essien in the playmaker role, to immense effect, and took the team to a Premier League and FA Cup Double in 2009-10.

‘He brought the best out of us and made us feel 10-feet tall,’ John Terry said of Ancelotti. ‘You know his training is excellent because he’s coached the best teams in the world – but it was the personal touches which lived with me.’

Ancelotti is coach of the Brazil team, of course, and it would be costly to extract him from that post, though there was always a sense with him of unfinished business in the Premier League. He was disappointed when Abramovich sacked him after two years and coveted the Liverpool job which Jurgen Klopp got instead.

Ancelotti led Chelsea to a Premier League and FA Cup Double in 2009-10 despite the meddling of Roman Abramovich

Ancelotti is coach of the Brazil team but there is a sense with him of unfinished business in the Premier League

The prospect of helicoptering around Howe and Nagelsmann, who would both be receptive to United, or Antonio Conte, whose temper would make him a disaster at the club, feels rather like Groundhog Day – taking United back to the dither of 2024, when they stuck with Erik ten Hag, despite the availability of the proven, far better Thomas Tuchel, whom they’d actually interviewed at that time.

Ancelotti or Howe to get a tune out of an imbalanced squad that has been patched up with cheaper options and, given the limited money available in January, will be little improved on? It’s obviously no contest.

In Ole Gunnar Solskjaer's time as manager from 2018, assistant manager Mike Phelan used to shout the word ‘standards’ down the corridors at the training ground and that’s what United need restoring again now.

More immediately come fixtures against Chelsea, Roma, Aston Villa, Liverpool, Sporting Lisbon and Brentford. It will require no little personality and authority to deal with that lot.

#1459. Cold activates a BH2 metabolic network promoting longevity and proteostasis

Thank you for visiting nature.com. You are using a browser version with limited support for CSS. To obtain the best experience, we recommend you use a more up to date browser (or turn off compatibility mode in Internet Explorer). In the meantime, to ensure continued support, we are displaying the site without styles and JavaScript.

Nature (2026) Cite this article

Moderately cold temperatures extend lifespan and promote health across species1,2,3, yet the molecular mechanisms underlying these benefits remain unclear. Here we define a metabolite-driven, multi-tissue signalling system that links cold temperature to enhanced longevity and proteostasis in Caenorhabditis elegans. Cold temperature upregulates the biopterin-synthesis enzyme CAT-4 (GCH1 in humans), increasing production of dihydrobiopterin (BH2), a transportable reservoir for the bioactive cofactor tetrahydrobiopterin (BH4). BH2 is produced in muscle and transported to distal tissues, including the intestine and neurons, where it is converted into BH4 by the salvage enzyme DHFR-1. BH4 functions as an essential cofactor for phenylalanine hydroxylase PAH-1, promoting tyrosine synthesis and extending lifespan. This BH2–BH4 axis also prevents disease-related protein aggregation and subsequent neuronal dysfunction during ageing. Importantly, increasing BH2 synthesis specifically in muscle is sufficient to promote longevity and suppress pathological protein aggregation in distal tissues, even at normal or elevated temperatures. In human cells, BH2 or tyrosine supplementation reduces aggregation of polyQ-expanded proteins associated with Huntington’s disease, as well as mutant variants of TDP-43 and FUS that cause amyotrophic lateral sclerosis. Together, these findings identify BH2 as a cold-induced metabolite of which the synthesis, intertissue transport and conversion into bioactive BH4 preserve organism-wide proteostasis and extend lifespan.

Emerging evidence demonstrates that moderately cold temperatures delay ageing and promote health across species1. Lowering body temperature extends lifespan in both poikilotherms, including C. elegans, Drosophila melanogaster and various fish species, and homeotherms, including rodents1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8. In adult C. elegans, a shift from standard (20 °C) to moderately low temperature (15 °C) markedly extends lifespan2,9,10,11,12. In rodents, a mild decrease in body temperature by only around 0.5 °C prolongs lifespan, whereas a comparable increase shortens it3,4. Lower body temperature also correlates with longevity in humans13,14. Notably, average human body temperature has declined since the Industrial Revolution, raising the possibility that changes in body temperature may have contributed to increased longevity over recent decades13. Together, these findings suggest that moderate cold engages evolutionarily conserved programs that promote longevity.

Despite being recognized for more than a century15, the mechanisms by which moderate cold temperature promotes longevity remain largely unclear. Historically, cold-induced lifespan extension was interpreted as a consequence of passive thermodynamic effects, including reduced metabolic rate and slower biochemical reaction kinetics. However, studies in C. elegans revealed that cold-induced longevity is an actively regulated process2,9,10,11,12. For example, the temperature-sensitive TRPA-1 ion channel promotes longevity at 15–20 °C but not at warmer temperatures, indicating that low temperature is sensed and transduced into longevity mechanisms11,12. Moreover, cold temperature induces transcriptional programs that enhance proteostasis2,9.

Despite these advances, a fundamental question remains of what systemic signals coordinate cold-induced responses across tissues. Small metabolites are ideally suited for this role because they can act as rapidly diffusible regulators integrating metabolism, enzymatic activity and intertissue signalling. We therefore hypothesized that cold temperature induces metabolite-based signals that coordinate metabolic, proteostatic and longevity pathways across tissues. We further hypothesized that such metabolites could function as temperature-independent effectors capable of recapitulating the beneficial effects of cold temperature even at higher temperatures.

Here we identify BH2 as a cold-induced metabolite of which the synthesis, intertissue transport and conversion into the bioactive enzymatic cofactor BH4 constitute a multi-tissue regulatory system that promotes longevity and maintains proteostasis across tissues. Through metabolomic, genetic and physiological analyses, we find that elevated BH2 synthesis in muscle and its conversion to BH4 in distal tissues promotes tyrosine synthesis, thereby mediating key aspects of cold-induced longevity. This pathway can be leveraged to confer longevity and proteostasis benefits at normal and elevated temperatures in both C. elegans and human cells.

Exposure of C. elegans to moderately low temperature (15 °C) after development induces a considerable extension of lifespan2,10,11,12. To assess metabolic changes associated with this effect, we raised wild-type worms at the standard temperature (20 °C) until adulthood and subsequently transferred them to 15 °C, 20 °C or 25 °C. Untargeted metabolomics analysis on day 5 of adulthood identified 64 metabolites that were significantly increased at 15 °C relative to both 20 °C and 25 °C (Fig. 1a and Supplementary Table 1). An independent experiment confirmed 45 of these metabolites, with strong concordance between datasets (Extended Data Fig. 1a–d and Supplementary Table 2).

a, Metabolomics of day 5 adult wild-type worms at 15 °C versus 20 °C (left) or 25 °C (right). n = 5 biological replicates. The volcano plots show log2-transformed fold change (log2[FC]) versus −log10-transformed FDR-adjusted P value (−log10[q]). The red dots indicate the 45 cold-induced metabolites increased at 15 °C versus both 20 °C and 25 °C in two independent experiments (FDR-adjusted P < 0.01; Supplementary Tables 1 and 2). b, Schematic of de novo, salvage and recycling BH4 synthesis pathways. Cold-induced metabolites are shown in red font; key enzymes required for cold-induced longevity are outlined by red boxes. C. elegans lacks sepiapterin reductase (SPR). The diagram was created using BioRender; Vilchez, D. https://BioRender.com/mno2d7j (2026). c, BH2 levels in adult wild-type worms across ages at 20 °C or 15 °C. Data are mean ± s.e.m. n = 5 biological replicates. d, BH2 levels in day 5 adult wild-type and trpa-1(ok999) mutant worms. Data are mean ± s.e.m. n = 5 biological replicates. e, Quantitative proteomics of day 5 adult wild-type worms at 15 °C versus 20 °C (above) or 25 °C (below). n = 3 biological replicates. The red dots indicate proteins commonly increased at 15 °C versus 20 °C and 25 °C (FDR < 0.05). f, BH4 levels in day 5 adult sterile fer-15;fem-1 worms treated after development with vector or cat-4 RNAi at 15 °C or 20 °C. Data are mean ± s.e.m., relative to 20 °C + vector RNAi. n = 8 independent experiments. g, cat-4 RNAi after development shortens the lifespan of wild-type worms at 15 °C (P < 0.0001) but not at 20 °C (P = 0.2595) or 25 °C (P = 0.8993). h, dhfr-1 RNAi after development shortens lifespan at 15 °C (P = 0.0005) but not at 20 °C (P = 0.7861). For g,h, n = 96 worms per condition; data are representative of two independent experiments. Data are mean ± s.e.m. and replicate data from independent lifespan experiments are provided in Supplementary Table 7. Statistical analysis was performed using two-sided t-tests with FDR correction (a,e), two-way analysis of variance (ANOVA) with Tukey’s multiple-comparison test (c,d) or Fisher’s least significant difference (LSD) test (f), and two-sided log-rank tests (g,h).

Among the 45 reproducibly cold-induced metabolites, several were linked to BH4 synthesis and its function as an enzymatic cofactor, including GTP, BH2, tryptophan, phenylalanine and tyrosine (Fig. 1a,b). As BH2 was among the most strongly induced metabolites at cold temperature (Fig. 1a), we focused subsequent analyses on this metabolite. Cold exposure after development increased BH2 levels from day 1 of adulthood, and BH2 remained elevated at 15 °C relative to age-matched animals at 20 °C until at least day 10 (Fig. 1c and Supplementary Table 3). BH2 induction was not a general consequence of longevity, as reduced insulin signalling, a distinct longevity pathway, did not increase BH2 levels at 20 °C (Extended Data Fig. 1e and Supplementary Table 4).

Loss of the temperature-sensitive trpa-1 channel shortens lifespan under both standard and cold temperatures11. However, worms still exhibit substantially extended lifespan at 15 °C compared with at 20 °C, indicating that cold-induced longevity is not solely mediated by TRPA-1-dependent pathways. Loss of trpa-1 did not reduce BH2 levels at cold temperature (Fig. 1d and Supplementary Table 1), demonstrating that elevated BH2 synthesis is induced independently of canonical TRPA-1 temperature sensing.

We next examined whether BH2 reflects a regulated pathway rather than a passive metabolic consequence of reduced temperature. Quantitative proteomics identified GTP cyclohydrolase I (GCH1 in humans, CAT-4 in C. elegans), the first and rate-limiting enzyme in BH2 synthesis16,17, as one of the most strongly induced proteins at 15 °C (Fig. 1e and Supplementary Table 5). Cold temperature also increased cat-4 mRNA amounts, indicating that CAT-4 induction occurs, at least in part, at the transcriptional level (Extended Data Fig. 2a). MED15 (human)/MDT-15 (C. elegans), a key transcriptional regulator of metabolic responses, is required for cold-induced longevity9. Importantly, knockdown of mdt-15 reduced cat-4 transcript levels at 15 °C (Extended Data Fig. 2b), indicating that cold-induced CAT-4 expression is part of a regulated transcriptional program.

Although BH2 is an inactive metabolite, it functions as a key intermediate in the synthesis and intertissue transport of its reduced, bioactive form, BH416,17. As BH4 is chemically unstable and readily oxidized, the more-stable BH2 form serves as a major organismal reservoir of this bioactive metabolite16,17. Consequently, cat-4-mutant worms are severely depleted of BH418. Consistent with the essential role of BH4 as a cofactor for multiple enzymes, these worms exhibit increased fragility compared with wild-type worms18. Accordingly, cat-4-mutant worms were short lived under both cold and standard temperatures (Extended Data Fig. 2c–e). Knockdown of cat-4 from early development also shortened lifespan at both 15 °C and 20 °C, although the effect was more pronounced under cold temperatures (Extended Data Fig. 2f).

As cold temperature during adulthood was sufficient to induce CAT-4 expression, BH2 synthesis and lifespan extension, we next examined the effects of cat-4 knockdown after development. As BH4 could not be detected by metabolomics because of its chemical instability, we quantified BH4 using an enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay (ELISA). Cold temperature induced a significant but modest increase in BH4 levels (Fig. 1f), consistent with BH2 serving as the major intermediate reservoir for this metabolite16,17. Knockdown of cat-4 after development decreased BH4 levels under both standard and cold temperatures, although this reduction was moderate (Fig. 1f). Importantly, cat-4 RNA interference (RNAi) decreased BH4 at cold temperature only to levels comparable to those observed at standard temperature without knockdown (Fig. 1f). Thus, BH4 levels remained within a physiological range, enabling us to determine whether elevated CAT-4 activity during adulthood is required for cold-induced longevity. Notably, knockdown of cat-4 after development diminished the lifespan extension induced by cold temperature, but had no significant effect on lifespan at 20 °C or 25 °C (Fig. 1g).

To further dissect the adult-specific requirement for elevated CAT-4 in cold-induced longevity, we generated an auxin-inducible degron (AID) strain enabling conditional degradation of CAT-4 protein in somatic tissues (Extended Data Fig. 2g). CAT-4 degradation from early development substantially reduced lifespan at 20 °C (Extended Data Fig. 2h). By contrast, degradation of CAT-4 after development did not affect lifespan at 20 °C, but specifically shortened longevity at 15 °C (Extended Data Fig. 2i). These results further indicate that CAT-4 activity is essential during development for adult viability, whereas increased CAT-4 activity during adulthood specifically contributes to cold-induced longevity.

Prompted by these findings, we next examined whether CAT-4 activity also contributes to lifespan extension in other longevity paradigms. Knockdown of cat-4 in mutant worms with reduced insulin signalling did not affect their extended lifespan at standard temperature, but only shortened lifespan at 15 °C (Extended Data Fig. 3a,b). By contrast, knockdown of cat-4 shortened lifespan in long-lived worms with reduced mitochondrial electron-transport chain activity or reduced food intake at both cold and standard temperatures (Extended Data Fig. 3c–h), suggesting that CAT-4 activity contributes to other longevity paradigms beyond cold temperature.

Downstream of activity of GCH1/CAT-4, bioactive BH4 can be generated through three pathways: the de novo, salvage and recycling pathways16,17 (Fig. 1b). In the de novo pathway, a GCH1/CAT-4-dependent intermediate is converted to BH4 by sepiapterin reductase. In the salvage pathway, this intermediate is instead converted to BH2 by either aldose reductase (AKR1B1 (human), Y39G8B.1 (C. elegans)) or carbonyl reductase 1 (CBR1 (human), DHS-31 (C. elegans))16,19. This pathway operates alongside de novo BH4 synthesis, but is particularly important in mammalian tissues with low sepiapterin reductase activity and in organisms such as C. elegans, which lack a sepiapterin reductase orthologue16,18. Knockdown of the BH2-producing enzyme Y39G8B.1 reduced lifespan specifically at 15 °C, whereas loss of dhs-31 produced a smaller, non-significant effect (Extended Data Fig. 4a). In the salvage pathway, BH2 is further converted into bioactive BH4 by dihydrofolate reductase (DHFR (human), DHFR-1 (C. elegans)). Importantly, knockdown of dhfr-1 after development decreased longevity at cold temperature but did not shorten lifespan at standard temperature (Fig. 1h). These results indicate that, downstream of CAT-4 activity, the conversion of BH2 into bioactive BH4 by DHFR-1 contributes to cold-induced longevity.

A recycling mechanism regenerates BH4 after its use as an enzymatic cofactor16,18 (Fig. 1b). In C. elegans, the recycling pathway is not required to maintain steady-state BH4 levels and becomes rate-limiting only under conditions of low BH4 synthesis, such as in reduction-of-function cat-4 mutants18. Subsequently, knockdown of recycling enzymes did not shorten the lifespan of wild-type worms at cold temperature (Extended Data Fig. 4b). Likewise, mutant worms defective in BH4 recycling were not short lived at 15 °C compared with wild-type worms (Extended Data Fig. 4c,d), indicating that this pathway is dispensable for cold-induced longevity. Together, our findings support a central role of CAT-4 activity and the salvage pathway in elevated BH4 synthesis for cold-induced longevity.

To determine the tissues in which CAT-4 is expressed, we generated a GFP-based fluorescent reporter driven by the endogenous cat-4 promoter. Knockdown of cat-4 reduced the GFP signal, validating the reporter (Extended Data Fig. 5a). At standard temperature, CAT-4 was mostly expressed in the anterior intestine, and cold exposure further increased CAT-4 levels in this region (Extended Data Fig. 5a,b). In contrast to the intestine, CAT-4 was barely detectable in muscle at 20 °C (Fig. 2a). However, cold temperature triggered robust expression of cat-4 in the muscle, an effect that was abolished by cat-4 RNAi treatment (Fig. 2a,b). To assess whether elevated BH2 synthesis in these tissues promotes organismal longevity at cold temperature, we performed tissue-specific knockdown experiments. Notably, knockdown of cat-4 in the muscle alone reduced cold-induced longevity, whereas knockdown in the intestine had no effect on lifespan (Fig. 2c,d). Likewise, knockdown of cat-4 in neurons, epidermis or germline did not shorten lifespan at 15 °C (Fig. 2e–g).

a, Representative images of day 5 adult worms expressing cat-4p::cat-4::SL2::GFP at the indicated temperatures. Cold temperature induces cat-4 expression in muscle cells. b, cat-4 RNAi after development abolishes cold-induced CAT-4 upregulation in muscle. In a,b, the arrows indicate CAT-4 in muscle. Scale bars, 20 µm (a,b). Images are representative of three independent experiments. c–g, Tissue-specific cat-4 knockdown (KD) after development. Muscle-specific knockdown shortens cold-induced longevity (c; P < 0.0001), whereas intestinal (d; P = 0.5374), neuronal (e; P = 0.8708), epidermal (f; P = 0.8339) or germline (g; P = 0.2604) knockdown does not. h, Single knockdown of ent-1 (P < 0.0001), ent-5 (P < 0.0001) or ent-6 (P < 0.0001), but not ent-4 (P = 0.9091), shortens lifespan at 15 °C. i, Knockdown of ent-1 (P = 0.2209), ent-5 (P = 0.9840) or ent-6 (P = 0.1278) does not shorten lifespan at 20 °C. j–n, Tissue-specific dhfr-1 RNAi after development. Muscle (j; P = 0.1076) or epidermal (k; P = 0.1830) knockdown does not affect cold-induced longevity, whereas neuronal (l; P < 0.0001), intestinal (m; P = 0.0001) or germline (n; P = 0.0001) knockdown reduces lifespan at 15 °C. In c–n, n = 96 worms per condition; data are representative of at least two independent experiments. Statistical comparisons were performed using two-sided log-rank tests. Data are mean ± s.e.m. Details of statistical analyses, and replicate data of lifespan experiments are provided in Supplementary Table 7.

To further investigate the link between cold-induced CAT-4 expression in muscle and longevity, we generated worms in which auxin-inducible degradation of CAT-4 was restricted to muscle. We confirmed that auxin treatment specifically depleted CAT-4 in the muscle of these animals, whereas ubiquitous somatic degradation reduced CAT-4 levels in both muscle and intestine (Extended Data Fig. 5c,d). Notably, muscle-specific degradation of CAT-4 after development was sufficient to fully phenocopy the longevity-shortening effects of ubiquitous somatic CAT-4 degradation at 15 °C (Extended Data Fig. 5e). This effect was specific to cold temperature, as muscle-specific CAT-4 degradation did not affect lifespan at standard temperature (Extended Data Fig. 5f). Together, these results identify muscle as a critical tissue in which elevated BH2 synthesis promotes cold-induced longevity.

BH4 is essential for the function of amino acid hydroxylases (TPH1 (human), TPH-1 (C. elegans); TH (human), CAT-2 (C. elegans); PAH (human), PAH-1 (C. elegans) and alkylglycerol monooxygenase (AGMO (human), AGMO-1 (C. elegans)). However, these enzymes are not expressed in muscle cells18, raising the possibility that BH4 acts in a cell non-autonomous manner. BH2 is the principal transport intermediate for BH4 distribution between tissues, enabling the rapid generation and utilization of bioactive BH4 in distal tissues16,17. We therefore hypothesized that cold temperature increases BH2 synthesis in muscle cells, which is then released and transported to distal tissues, where it is converted into BH4 to activate its target enzymes. In humans, the uptake of BH2 is mediated by the transmembrane nucleoside transporters ENT1 (SLC29A1) and ENT2 (SLC29A2)16. C. elegans expresses seven orthologues of human ENT1 and ENT2 (ent-1 to ent-7). We found that single knockdown of different orthologues, such as ent-1, ent-5 and ent-6, specifically shortens lifespan at 15 °C but not 20 °C (Fig. 2h,i).

Once BH2 is taken up by distal cells, it can be converted into bioactive BH4 through the salvage pathway by the DHFR-1 enzyme16,20. Consequently, BH4 redistribution efficiency depends on DHFR-1 activity, which regulates local BH4 availability. While BH2 synthesis through CAT-4 activity in muscle cells is required for cold-induced longevity (Fig. 2c), we found that muscle-specific knockdown of dhfr-1 does not shorten lifespan at 15 °C (Fig. 2j). Similarly, dhfr-1 knockdown in the epidermis had no effect on longevity at 15 °C (Fig. 2k). By contrast, tissue-specific knockdown of dhfr-1 in neurons, intestine and germline reduced cold-induced longevity (Fig. 2l–n). Moreover, neither neuron-specific knockdown of dhfr-1 nor muscle-specific knockdown of cat-4 further reduced the shortened lifespan of ent-1 mutant animals at cold temperature (Extended Data Fig. 5g,h). These results support the notion that cold temperature induces BH2 synthesis in muscle cells, after which, BH2 is released and converted to bioactive BH4 in distal tissues such as neurons, intestine and germline, where it promotes cold-induced longevity.

To further examine the cell non-autonomous effects of BH2, we focused on the intestine, a tissue that progressively loses structural integrity during ageing21,22. To this end, we assessed the intermediate filament IFB-2 because this protein is expressed exclusively in the intestine and undergoes age-related changes that can be readily monitored22. In young animals, IFB-2 is confined to the apical cytoplasm of intestinal cells. With age, IFB-2 levels increase and the protein becomes mislocalized throughout the cytoplasm, where it forms aggregates that contribute to intestinal decline22. We found that cold temperature prevents IFB-2 mislocalization and aggregation during ageing (Extended Data Fig. 6a). However, loss of cat-4 abolished these protective effects (Extended Data Fig. 6a). Notably, muscle-specific knockdown of cat-4 was sufficient to block the cold-induced suppression of intestinal IFB-2 aggregation (Extended Data Fig. 6b). Collectively, these findings indicate that cold-induced BH2 synthesis in muscle exerts cell non-autonomous effects, such as preserving intestinal integrity during ageing.

BH2 supplementation is often more effective at increasing BH4 levels than direct BH4 administration16,20. However, C. elegans exhibits a limited capacity for biopterin uptake, including BH2, and exogenous supplementation results in only a modest increase in biopterin levels18. Despite this limitation, BH2 supplementation in adult worms led to a slight but significant extension of cold-induced longevity (Extended Data Fig. 7a). Although BH2 supplementation did not extend lifespan at standard temperature, it delayed the age-related decline in motility, a key indicator of healthspan (Extended Data Fig. 7b,c). Although BH2-treated worms did not display overt signs of sickness, we further assessed the possibility that these beneficial effects arise from a hormetic stress response, such as that induced by mitochondrial dysfunction23,24. However, BH2 supplementation did not reduce brood size and did not induce the mitochondrial unfolded protein response (Extended Data Fig. 7d,e), two commonly used indicators of physiological stress.

Given the limited uptake of exogenous biopterins in worms, we used a genetic approach to assess the full potential of these metabolites to extend longevity. Ubiquitous overexpression of cat-4 in somatic tissues resulted in a substantial increase in cold-induced longevity (Extended Data Fig. 7f). Notably, overexpression of cat-4 in the muscle alone was sufficient to increase organismal levels of bioactive BH4 and extend lifespan not only at 15 °C, but also at 20 °C and 25 °C (Fig. 3a,b and Extended Data Fig. 7g–i). Moreover, muscle-specific cat-4 overexpression also extended lifespan in loss-of-function trpa-1 mutant worms across different temperatures (Extended Data Fig. 7j–l). These findings indicate that increasing CAT-4 activity in muscle promotes longevity independently of both temperature and the cold-sensitive channel TRPA-1.

a,b, Muscle-specific cat-4 overexpression (OE) extends lifespan at 15 °C and 20 °C (a; P < 0.0001) and at 25 °C (b; P < 0.0001). c, Filter-trap analysis of intestinal IFB-2 aggregation (detected by anti-IFB-2 antibody) in control and muscle-specific cat-4-overexpressing worms at 20 °C on days 1 and 10 of adulthood. Right, SDS–PAGE and immunoblotting of IFB-2 and the α-tubulin loading control. Images are representative of three independent experiments; quantification is shown in Extended Data Fig. 7m. d, Representative images of intestinal colonization by mCherry-expressing Escherichia coli in control and muscle-specific cat-4-overexpressing worms on days 1 and 10. Scale bar, 200 µm. Representative of three independent experiments. DIC, differential interference contrast. e, Quantification of intestinal bacterial colonization at 20 °C. The box plots represent the 25th–75th percentiles, the lines depict the median and the whiskers show the minimum–maximum values. Day 1: control, n = 33 worms; muscle cat-4(OE), n = 24; day 10: control, n = 23; muscle cat-4(OE), n = 27, from three independent experiments. Statistical analysis was performed using two-way ANOVA with Fisher’s LSD test. f,g, In addition to muscle-specific overexpression, intestinal and neuronal cat-4 overexpression also extends longevity at cold (15 °C; f) and standard (20 °C; g) temperature. P < 0.0001 for muscle, intestinal and neuronal overexpression at 15 °C; P < 0.0001 for muscle and intestinal overexpression at 20 °C; and P = 0.0121 for neuronal overexpression at 20 °C. In a,b,f,g, n = 96 worms per condition; data are representative of at least two independent experiments. Statistical analysis was performed using two-sided log-rank tests. Data are mean ± s.e.m. Details of the statistical analyses and replicate data of lifespan experiments are provided in Supplementary Table 7. Gel source data are provided in Supplementary Fig. 1.

Consistent with a systemic protective effect, muscle-specific cat-4 overexpression prevented age-related accumulation and aggregation of intestinal IFB-2 protein at standard temperature (Fig. 3c and Extended Data Fig. 7m). As IFB-2 aggregation contributes to loss of intestinal integrity and subsequent bacterial colonization during ageing22, we examined whether CAT-4 prevents this deleterious process. Indeed, muscle-specific cat-4 overexpression reduced bacterial invasion of the intestine in aged worms at standard temperature (Fig. 3d,e). We then tested whether muscle-derived BH2 also acts in neurons. Neuronal-specific downregulation of either BH2 uptake (using ent-1 RNAi) or its conversion to BH4 through the salvage pathway (through dhfr-1 RNAi) significantly reduced the lifespan extension conferred by muscle-specific cat-4 overexpression at standard temperature, but had no effect in control worms (Extended Data Fig. 7n,o). These results further demonstrate that elevated BH2 production in muscle extends lifespan in a cell non-autonomous manner and requires both BH2 uptake and its conversion into bioactive BH4 in distal tissues, including the intestine and neurons.

We next examined whether increasing CAT-4 levels directly in the tissues that use BH2 is sufficient to promote longevity. While muscle-specific cat-4 overexpression had the strongest longevity effect, intestinal overexpression also markedly extended lifespan at both 15 °C and 20 °C (Fig. 3f,g). Although to a lesser extent, cat-4 overexpression in neurons also increased lifespan under both cold and standard temperatures (Fig. 3f,g). Together, these data suggest that, while elevated BH2 synthesis in muscle has a central role in longevity, increasing cat-4 expression in other tissues partially mimics its beneficial effects.

To determine how utilization of BH4 as an enzymatic cofactor contributes to cold-induced longevity, we performed a targeted RNAi screen against the four BH4-dependent enzymes expressed in C. elegans: phenylalanine hydroxylase (PAH-1), tryptophan hydroxylase (TPH-1), tyrosine hydroxylase (CAT-2) and alkylglycerol monooxygenase (AGMO-1)18. Among them, knockdown of pah-1 after development shortened lifespan, whereas knockdown of the other BH4-dependent enzymes did not affect cold-induced longevity (Extended Data Fig. 8a). Consistently, loss-of-function tph-1, cat-2 and agmo-1 mutants were not short-lived, whereas pah-1 mutants exhibited reduced longevity specifically at cold temperature (Extended Data Fig. 8b–d).

Among the tissues involved in cold-induced longevity, we found that PAH-1 activity in the intestine is particularly important for lifespan extension (Fig. 4a and Extended Data Fig. 8e–g). Consistent with this, knockdown of pah-1 increased both the levels and aggregation of the intestinal intermediate filament IFB-2, thereby abolishing the protective effects of cold temperature on age-associated IFB-2 aggregation (Fig. 4b and Extended Data Fig. 8h). While muscle-specific overexpression of cat-4 was sufficient to prevent IFB-2 aggregation during ageing at standard temperature, this protective effect was lost after pah-1 knockdown (Fig. 4c and Extended Data Fig. 8i).

a, Intestinal pah-1 RNAi after development shortens cold-induced longevity (P < 0.0001). b, Filter-trap analysis of intestinal IFB-2 aggregation in day 10 adult wild-type worms at 20 °C and 15 °C after vector control or pah-1 RNAi. Right, SDS–PAGE and immunoblotting of IFB-2 and the α-tubulin loading control. Images are representative of three independent experiments; quantification is shown in Extended Data Fig. 8h. c, Filter-trap analysis of IFB-2 aggregation in day 10 adult control and muscle-specific cat-4-overexpressing worms at 20 °C. Right, SDS–PAGE and immunoblotting of IFB-2 and α-tubulin. Images are representative of three independent experiments; quantification is shown in Extended Data Fig. 8i. d,e, Tyrosine supplementation after development extends lifespan at 15 °C (d; 0.5 mM, P = 0.0103; 1 mM, P = 0.0006) and 20 °C (e; 0.5 mM, P < 0.0001; 1 mM, P < 0.0001). The vehicle control was HCl. f, Knockdown of hpd-1 after development shortens lifespan at 15 °C (P < 0.0001) but not at 20 °C (P = 0.4504) or 25 °C (P = 0.0826). In lifespan assays, n = 96 worms per condition; data are representative of at least two independent experiments. Statistical comparisons were performed using two-sided log-rank tests. Data are mean ± s.e.m. Replicate data and statistical analyses of lifespan experiments are provided in Supplementary Table 7. Gel source data are provided in Supplementary Fig. 1.

PAH-1 catalyses the synthesis of tyrosine, a metabolite that we found to be upregulated at cold temperature (Fig. 1a). Exogenous supplementation with tyrosine extended lifespan not only at cold temperature, but also at standard temperature (Fig. 4d,e). Importantly, tyrosine supplementation rescued the reduction in cold-induced longevity caused by cat-4 knockdown (Extended Data Fig. 8j), further supporting a functional role of regulated tyrosine metabolism in mediating the longevity effects of elevated BH2 synthesis.

To gain insights into the downstream mechanisms by which tyrosine promotes longevity, we performed over-representation analysis (ORA) of the differentially abundant proteins in worms at 15 °C relative to either 20 °C or 25 °C (Supplementary Table 5). Notably, we found strong enrichment for proteins involved in small-molecule and amino acid metabolic processes (Extended Data Fig. 9a,b). Within these pathways, we identified increased levels of 4-hydroxyphenylpyruvate dioxygenase (HPD (human), HPD-1 (C. elegans); Extended Data Fig. 9c)—a key enzyme in the canonical tyrosine catabolic pathway that ultimately generates acetoacetate and fumarate, thereby linking tyrosine metabolism to central carbon metabolism25. Moreover, analysis of published transcriptomic datasets also revealed increased hpd-1 mRNA levels at cold temperature12 (Extended Data Fig. 9c). We found that loss of hpd-1 significantly reduced cold-induced longevity (Fig. 4f), supporting a functional contribution of tyrosine catabolism to lifespan extension at low temperature. Although hpd-1 knockdown also caused a slight decrease in lifespan at 20 °C and 25 °C, these effects did not reach statistical significance (Fig. 4f).

To identify molecular pathways that are responsive to elevated tyrosine levels, we performed quantitative proteomic analyses after tyrosine supplementation at both 15 °C and 20 °C (Supplementary Table 6). These analyses revealed 54 commonly upregulated proteins and 42 commonly downregulated proteins after tyrosine supplementation across both temperatures (Extended Data Fig. 9d,e). ORA of the differentially abundant proteins identified significant enrichment for lipid metabolic processes and enzyme regulator activity (Extended Data Fig. 9f,g). Together, these data suggest that tyrosine promotes longevity through specific downstream regulatory and metabolic pathways, including HPD-1-dependent tyrosine catabolism.

Loss of proteostasis during ageing is a major risk factor for diseases characterized by pathological protein aggregation26. Moderately cold temperatures suppress disease-related protein aggregation across tissues, including polyQ-expanded proteins in C. elegans models of Huntington’s disease2,9. Knockdown of cat-4 abolished this protective effect and increased aggregation without altering total polyQ levels (Extended Data Fig. 10a–c), indicating that CAT-4 activity modulates aggregation rather than overall protein amounts.

Consistent with the cell non-autonomous effects of muscle CAT-4 activity, loss of cat-4 increased aggregation at cold temperature not only in muscle, but also in worms expressing polyQ-expanded proteins exclusively in the intestine or neurons (Extended Data Fig. 10a–c). In neurons, polyQ67 aggregation causes behavioural deficits during ageing, including impaired chemotaxis and nose-touch avoidance compared with wild-type or polyQ19 controls27,28. Cold temperature partially rescued these neuronal deficits, whereas loss of cat-4 abolished this protection (Extended Data Fig. 10d,e). Conversely, muscle-specific overexpression of cat-4 reduced neuronal polyQ67 aggregation under both standard (20 °C) and warmer (25 °C) temperatures, preventing deficits in chemotaxis and nose-touch avoidance (Extended Data Fig. 10f–h). These results support a cell non-autonomous role for muscle CAT-4 activity in maintaining proteostasis in distal tissues.

We next investigated how elevated CAT-4 activity enhances proteostasis capacity. Cold temperature induces the proteasome activator PA28γ (human, encoded by PSME3)/PSME-3 (C. elegans), increasing trypsin-like proteasome activity2. Knockdown of psme-3 attenuated the ability of muscle-specific cat-4 overexpression to suppress neuronal polyQ67 aggregation, without altering total polyQ levels (Extended Data Fig. 11a), suggesting a broader defect in proteostasis rather than impaired degradation of polyQ proteins per se. Thus, CAT-4 activity appears to influence proteostasis through additional mechanisms. Besides proteasome activity, cold temperature also induces expression of different subunits of the TRiC/CCT complex10, a chaperonin that prevents polyQ-expanded aggregation29,30,31. Knockdown of cct-2 subunit completely abolished the protective effects of muscle-specific cat-4 overexpression at standard temperature (Extended Data Fig. 11b). By contrast, knockdown of fkb-6, a cold-induced co-chaperone of HSP9010, had no effect on polyQ67 aggregation (Extended Data Fig. 11c). We also tested hsf-1, a key transcription factor that protects against protein misfolding and aggregation32. Loss of hsf-1 partially attenuated the protective effects of muscle-specific cat-4 overexpression (Extended Data Fig. 11d), although less strongly than cct-2 or psme-3 knockdown.

Finally, we assessed whether PAH-1 activity contributes to these proteostasis mechanisms. Indeed, loss of pah-1 reduced the cold-induced upregulation of cct-2 and psme-3 expression, as well as the increase in trypsin-like proteasome activity (Extended Data Fig. 11e–g). Together, these results indicate that elevated CAT-4 activity and subsequent BH4-dependent tyrosine synthesis enhance proteostasis through different downstream mechanisms, including proteasome activity and the TRiC/CCT chaperonin complex.

We next examined whether BH2 supplementation prevents disease-related protein aggregation in human cells at standard temperature (37 °C). To this end, we generated HEK293 cells expressing mutant huntingtin exon 1 containing polyQ74 repeats, which is prone to aggregation33,34 (Fig. 5a). We found that supplementation with 10 µM BH2 markedly reduces polyQ aggregation without affecting overall protein levels (Fig. 5a). To determine whether BH2 also protects against aggregation of other disease-associated proteins, we assessed mutant variants of TDP-43 and FUS associated with amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS). In contrast to their wild-type counterparts, the ALS-causing mutant TDP-43(A382T) and FUS(P525L) variants aggregated in HEK293 human cells (Fig. 5b,c). However, BH2 supplementation prevented aggregation of both mutant proteins (Fig. 5b,c). Consistent with this mechanism, exogenous tyrosine supplementation also reduced pathological protein aggregation (Fig. 5d).

a, Filter-trap and SDS–PAGE analysis of mRFP-HTTexon1-Q74 in HEK293 cells at standard (37 °C) temperature. Cells were treated with 10 µM BH2 or vehicle control (DMSO) for 3 days. The graphs show aggregated Q74 and total Q74 levels (normalized to the β-actin loading control) relative to the vehicle control. Data are mean ± s.e.m. n = 3 independent experiments. b,c, Filter-trap and SDS–PAGE analysis of aggregated and total TDP-43(A382T) (b) or FUS(P525L) (c) in HEK293 cells treated with 10 µM BH2 or vehicle control for 3 days. Quantifications are relative to the corresponding wild-type (WT) + vehicle control. Data are mean ± s.e.m. n = 3 independent experiments. d, Filter-trap and SDS–PAGE analysis of aggregated and total mRFP-HTTexon1-Q74 in HEK293 cells treated with the indicated concentrations of tyrosine or vehicle control (HCl) for 3 days. Data are mean ± s.e.m. relative to the vehicle control. n = 3 independent experiments. e,f, Immunocytochemistry analysis of mutant FUSP525L/P525L and isogenic control FUSWT/WT iPS-cell-derived motor neurons treated with 10 µM BH2 (e) or tyrosine (f) for 3 days. Cleaved caspase-3 (red), MAP2 (green) and Hoechst (blue) staining mark apoptosis, neurons and nuclei, respectively. Scale bars, 20 µm (e,f). The box plots show the 25th–75th percentiles (box limits), median (centre line) and minimum–maximum values (whiskers) of the percentage of cleaved-caspase-3-positive cells among MAP2-positive cells; n = 15 biological replicates from three independent experiments. g, Western blot analysis of phosphorylated RIP (P-RIP) at Ser166, total RIP and β-actin in wild-type and FUSP525L/P525L iPS-cell-derived motor neurons treated with 10 µM BH2. Data are mean ± s.e.m. relative to FUSP525L/P525L + vehicle control. n = 3 independent experiments. In a–g, images are representative of three independent experiments. Statistical comparisons were performed using two-sided unpaired t-tests (a,g), two-way ANOVA with Fisher’s LSD test (b,c,e,f) and one-way ANOVA with Dunnett’s multiple-comparison test (d). Gel source data are provided in Supplementary Fig. 1.

Prompted by these findings, we examined whether BH2 attenuates disease-related neurodegeneration in human cells. Motor neurons differentiated from induced pluripotent stem (iPS) cells expressing the severe ALS-linked FUS(P525L) mutation exhibit elevated apoptosis compared with isogenic control cells35. Notably, supplementation with either BH2 or tyrosine reduced apoptosis in ALS neurons (Fig. 5e,f). ALS motor neurons also showed elevated phosphorylation of receptor-interacting protein (RIP) kinase, a marker of necroptotic cell death, which was reduced by BH2 supplementation (Fig. 5g), further supporting a protective effect against disease-related neurodegeneration.

We then examined whether BH2 and tyrosine enhance proteostasis capacity through mechanisms similar to those activated by cold temperature in C. elegans. Indeed, both treatments caused a slight but significant increase in trypsin-like proteasome activity in HEK293 cells at standard temperature (Extended Data Fig. 12a,b). By contrast, neither treatment increased LC3 lipidation, a marker of autophagosomes (Extended Data Fig. 12c,d). As BH2 suppressed aggregation without significantly altering total levels of the pathological proteins (Fig. 5a–c), we examined additional proteostasis mechanisms. Consistent with our findings in worms, knockdown of the TRiC/CCT chaperonin subunit CCT2 reduced the ability of BH2 to suppress pathological protein aggregation, identifying TRiC/CCT-complex-dependent folding capacity as a key contributor to BH2-mediated proteostasis enhancement in human cells (Extended Data Fig. 12e).

In contrast to in C. elegans, mammalian cells express nitric oxide synthases (NOSs), which require BH4 as a cofactor16,18. Under conditions that decrease intracellular BH4 availability, such as oxidative stress—which promotes excessive oxidation of BH4 to BH2—or impaired DHFR activity, BH2 can compete with BH4 for binding to endothelial NOS (eNOS) in the vascular system36,37,38. This shifts eNOS activity from nitric oxide production toward superoxide generation, a process known as eNOS uncoupling38. Importantly, eNOS uncoupling is associated primarily with reduced BH4 availability and a consequent decrease in the BH4/BH2 ratio, rather than with elevated BH2 levels per se or BH2 supplementation. In fact, when cells retain an intact DHFR-dependent salvage pathway, BH2 supplementation increases intracellular BH4 levels and alleviates eNOS uncoupling under oxidative stress36,37. In contrast to paraquat-induced oxidative stress, neither 10 µM BH2 nor a 20-fold higher concentration increased the percentage of cells with detectable superoxide levels (Extended Data Fig. 12f). Moreover, even high doses of BH2 reduced pathological protein aggregation (Extended Data Fig. 12g), supporting the proteostasis benefits of BH2 supplementation without detectable superoxide production indicative of BH4 imbalance.

Given that oxidative stress promotes BH4 oxidation to BH2, we hypothesized that such conditions might limit the therapeutic potential of BH2 supplementation. Indeed, paraquat-induced oxidative stress abolished the protective effect of BH2 on protein aggregation (Extended Data Fig. 12h). By contrast, tyrosine supplementation retained its protective effect under oxidative stress (Extended Data Fig. 12i), suggesting that this downstream effector of the pathway can bypass conditions in which BH2 cannot be efficiently converted into BH4.

Here we identify BH2 as a cold-induced metabolite that links low temperature to systemic regulation of longevity and proteostasis (Extended Data Fig. 12j). Moderate cold increases CAT-4-dependent BH2 synthesis in muscle, from which BH2 is transported to distal tissues and converted into bioactive BH4 by DHFR-1. This establishes muscle as a metabolic hub that produces a pro-longevity signal of which the activity can be regulated locally in recipient tissues. BH4-dependent PAH-1 activity promotes tyrosine synthesis, and tyrosine supplementation recapitulates the longevity benefits of this pathway even at normal temperature, identifying the PAH-1–tyrosine axis as an important downstream mediator of the cold-induced BH2 pathway. The pathway also enhances organismal proteostasis: increasing BH2 synthesis in muscle suppresses pathological protein aggregation and preserves neuronal and intestinal function in distal tissues. Together, these findings expand the concept of cold-induced longevity beyond a predominantly cell-autonomous response to a systemic metabolic communication mechanism that coordinates longevity and proteostasis across tissues.

The ability of BH2 and tyrosine to suppress disease-associated protein aggregation and neurodegeneration-related phenotypes in human cells further supports functional conservation of this pathway. However, its translational potential may depend on the cellular context in which BH2 is converted to BH4. In mammals, BH4 is also required by NOS, and oxidative stress or impaired DHFR activity can disrupt the BH4/BH2 balance and promote eNOS uncoupling in endothelial cells36,37,38. BH2 supplementation did not increase detectable superoxide production in our cellular models, even at high concentrations, although these experiments do not directly address potential effects on NOS uncoupling in vivo. Consistent with the importance of BH2-to-BH4 conversion, we found that oxidative stress limits the protective effect of BH2, whereas tyrosine retains its protective effect. Thus, tyrosine may provide an alternative means of engaging this pathway when BH2-to-BH4 conversion is impaired. More broadly, our metabolomic analyses identified numerous additional metabolites induced by cold, raising the possibility that BH2 is one component of a larger network of systemic metabolic signals that coordinates the beneficial effects of low temperature. Defining these pathways may reveal additional strategies to reproduce the health-promoting effects of cold without altering body temperature.

All of the experiments were performed using hermaphrodite adult worms at the ages indicated in the corresponding figures and figure legends.

The control DVG9 strain (N2, ocbEx9[myo3p::GFP]) was generated by microinjecting 20 ng μl−1 pPD93_97 (myo-3p::GFP) into the gonads of adult N2 hermaphrodite animals47. To generate DVG348 (N2, ocbEx280[sur-5p::cat-4, myo-3p::GFP]), we injected a DNA mixture containing 70 ng μl−1 of the sur5-p::cat-4 plasmid and 5 ng μl−1 pPD93_97 (myo3-p::GFP). Following this method, we generated the strains DVG349 (intestine-specific overexpression: N2, ocbEx281[gly-19p::cat-4, myo-3p::GFP]), DVG351 (neuron-specific overexpression: N2, ocbEx283[rgef-1p::cat-4, myo-3p::GFP]), and DVG352 (muscle-specific overexpression: N2, ocbEx284[myo-3p::cat-4, myo-3p::GFP]).
